ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u27	The President of the First Permanent Commission, Senator Finocchiaro, may speak to report on the Commission's work.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u28	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the draft constitutional law, which is now before the Assembly, concerning the reform of Part II of the Constitution, was approved at first reading by the Senate on 8 August 2014 and at first reading by the House of Deputies on 10 March 2015. Since the text had been amended at several points during the first reading examination at the other branch of Parliament, the Senate carried out a second reading, again in the context of the first resolution, and in turn made further amendments. After the second reading of the Senate, concluded on October 13, 2015, the Chamber of Deputies again examined the constitutional bill, in this case approving it - on January 11 last - in the identical text transmitted by the Senate. The outcome of the double resolution in accordance with the Constitution has therefore reached the stage of the second resolution, as provided for in Article 138 of the Constitution, which - as is well known - regulates the constitutional revision procedure. On 12 January the draft Constitutional Law was then assigned to the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, which, on that same date, decided by a majority to place the measure on the agenda and to begin its examination from the next sitting. The Conference of Leaders, on 13 January, took the decision to include the draft Constitutional Review Act in the timetable for work since today's session of the Assembly. I have therefore tried to organise the debate in the Commission, in order to ensure that the debate could take place in a successful manner, so as to allow a positive conclusion to be made of the examination by conferring the mandate on the rapporteur. The constitutional bill was therefore incardinated into the Commission on 14 January. The general debate began in yesterday's afternoon sitting, on Monday 18 January, and continued in yesterday's afternoon and night sessions (sittings, however - it should be remembered - have not been fully exploited by colleagues for their speeches and therefore both stopped early) and in today's sitting, convened at 9 a.m. and concluded a few minutes ago. There were 45 senators in the debate. I would remind you that during the first reading, 68 senators took part in the general discussion in the Commission and 34 senators at second reading, today 45. However, at the very beginning of the sitting of the Assembly, there were still 15 members to speak. I have therefore been unable to take note of the fact that it is not possible to conclude the examination in a positive way, despite all efforts made to ensure that there is a greater availability of time for discussion in the Commission.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u29	In relation to President Finocchiaro's report, the draft law No 1429-D, since the examination has not been completed in the Commission, will be discussed without report. Requests for general discussion were received from 93 senators. The Presidency will ensure that the deadlines laid down in the Rules of Procedure are strictly respected. The order of the members is posted on the bulletin board. The senator who is not present in this Chamber will be deemed to have given up unless he has agreed with other senators and communicated it to the Presidency in good time. As you know, the sitting doesn't have a closing time.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u30	Mr President, having to deal with a subject such as constitutional matter, which I cannot separate from electoral matters, because they represent a particularly dangerous unicum, and having recognized in these two reforms of the pedestal characteristics (in the sense that they are written with my feet), I believe that the tone of my speech must also be of a didactic nature and bring everything back to certain concepts that have led our country to a real risk to democracy. I therefore start from the electoral subject, with considerations regarding the same that remind us all that electoral systems are those that translate votes, that is, preferences, into polling stations. What are the characteristics of the various systems? Characteristics of proportionality, that is, there is an exact coincidence between the votes cast and the seats assigned, which have the guarantee of maximum democracy and representativeness, and characteristics peculiar to the majority systems, that is those that tend to limit or exclude the presence of the minority, through the single or plurinomial colleges; what is certain is that the latter have the characteristic of giving the governability and stability of government, but are incomplete on the side of representativeness and democracy. To balance these four requirements mixed systems have been invented: the majoritarians such as the Mattarellum, which awarded 75% of the votes on the basis of single-member colleges, corrected with 25% proportional, and the Porcellum, which had a proportional system with a majority prize and barricades as bonus criteria. What doesn't work in this electoral law? It's not a mixed system. A particular form of the single system has been used, compared to a plurinomial system, using the ballot and, therefore, creating not a mixed system but a kind of mad mayonnaise. Everyone must recognize that there is no perfect electoral system; it proves that, over the course of all these years, in the face of a bicameral equal system that we have so far, with no electoral law the same majority would have been determined at Chamber and Senate level. It happened with the Mattarellum in 1996, it happened in 2006 with the Porcellum and it happened again this time; and all, once again, to bring the instability of government back to a responsibility of the so-called Porcellum law. It is good to remind everyone that no electoral system in the Western world would produce the same majority at the House and Senate level. It is a physiological result and is not so much attributable to the fact that the Senate also expresses confidence, but because, having two different electorates (for the House of Eighteen vote people who are twenty-five years old and over while for the Senate twenty-five years old and beyond vote for people who have turned 40), it is evident that, with a variation of the electorate active and passive, the same result cannot be allowed. In addition to trying to simulate the result of 2013, we tried to apply the various systems to see what would happen to the Chamber of Deputies alone. Curious results have emerged, even paradoxical: if we had voted in 2013 with the Mattarellum, a system that was quite opposed by the center-right and loved by the center-left, it would have won the Berlusconi elections and the center-right; if we had voted with the Spanish system, loved by the center-right and instead opposed by the center-left, it would have won the left and with the Greek system they would have even won the pentastellates with more than 250 votes. These numbers tell us that the possible majority would have been from 230 to 250 and therefore absolutely not able to produce a majority of government even at the level of the Chamber of Deputies. So there is not only a system and a problem of electoral laws and bicameralism, there is the problem that all these systems, created by bipolar realities, have been implemented and applied today to a reality that is being realized tripolar (and it is not said that the number of political formations can not even increase). Let us retrace the story of how we have come here, partly because I have been asked to do so today in the Commission. I have always had a special appreciation for the method of the so-called Mattarellum, that is, the electoral system approved in 1993, but we must also be not hypocrites and recognize how things really went. I have said this in the past and I repeat: as far as I am concerned, I would never have changed the electoral system of 1993. It was an imperfect system, precisely because when we adopt a law, before using it we find the deception, and that someone had put it as an element of detour, in order to switch from the single-name system to proportional, not the number of votes obtained by the winner, but those obtained by the second-ranked one made the larger parties over-represented also in the proportional part, as well as the system of owl lists, for which one connected to a ghost list, made Parliament no longer have the guarantee of the plenum, which was done two legislative acts ago. Faced with this, however, it would have been enough to correct the Mattarellum. But in 2005, just as at this moment we are moving on to carry out the third and fourth votes of the constitutional reform (and for the then UDC I asked myself, compared to the critical analyses of the New Right Center, why they did not do the same this time) came the blackmail of Fini, Casini and Follini, who said that if we had not done a reform in a proportional sense they would not have voted on the Constitution. That's why I had to change an electoral law that I would never, otherwise, change. I propose a proportional system that, above a certain threshold, determined the triggering of a majority prize and I think that was absolutely reasonable. Too bad that then there was no party secretary of the Party of the Nation, then there were really secretaries of parties that mattered something and everyone wanted a piece of that electoral law. The good Berlusconi asked that there was no minimum threshold above which to trigger the majority prize, which from a constitutional point of view is unsustainable, but then the two sides that faced each other arrived, even the one that lost, close to fifty percent, i.e. they won and lost with 49.5 compared to 50.05 percent, so the majority prize never exceeded 5 percent and was therefore absolutely reasonable. The good Fini instead wanted the lists blocked. There is something that does not come back: the whole world goes in a certain direction and instead we went in scattered order. What is certain is that bad, bad, good or not good preferences have behavior, within the country that risk being evidence of distorting effects. In the Lombardy Region, during the last elections, the population expressed 14% of preferences. In Naples, the expression of preferences in the municipal elections reaches 96 percent. That there is all this love only in Naples to express the preference and that there is not in Milan leads me to think that fears over what Fini claimed could also be real. I accepted that request not only for blackmail but also because there was the promise from all the others that the country would then be divided, not in the 27 districts of today and then, but in at least 200, so that the lists were two or three people, and therefore absolutely identifiable by the population. Then he also placed his Presidency of the Republic, claiming that for the election on a regional basis, constitutionally planned for the Senate, the majority prize should also be awarded at regional level. Even the smaller parties came forward - without naming names - and someone came to question two percent of the barrier, saying that even if they had gone under, the first of the losers would still enter Parliament. Here, in front of all these requests I came out with the definition of the famous law "porcata." I said "porcata" then because I had not yet seen what you approved then, because otherwise I would never have called it "porcata." [[Applause from the LN-Aut Group and Senator Campanella]] : It is a lily with respect to the law that you approved. Yet, if the porcellum approved in 2005 was so indisputable, someone wants to unravel the hypocrisy that, even though a left-wing government, a right-wing government, a technical government and one of enlarged majorities, no one has changed that law? To this no one has yet given an answer, because everyone criticizes him, but party secretaries would never have changed the Porcellum if the Constitutional Court's ruling had not arrived. I call it a spontaneous decision of the Constitutional Court because it has always surprised me that the Constitutional Court had already dealt with the issue twice, in the course of the same repealing referendums, and, although it had criticized certain passages of the law, it had always concluded that it is up to Parliament to legislate on electoral matters. This time, perhaps spontaneously urged by President Napolitano, the constitutional judges entered into the matter and went to censor two passages of the Porcellum; let's see which. First of all, the unreasonableness of the majority prize: they are absolutely right, but the historical period is a different thing. Then, the excessive length of the lists: absolutely in agreement; but if the electoral colleges had been revised, the problem would not have arisen. I accept the censure on the unreasonableness of the majority prize and also on the blocked lists, but I wonder how the Constitutional Court's ruling could have introduced, by using secondary and even regulatory legislation, one or more preferences. This is something I still cannot frankly explain. [[Applause from the LN-Aut Group]] It is clear that good Renzi and Minister Boschi were looking forward to having the ball raised by the Constitutional Court to change the electoral law. Everyone knew the good parts and the bad parts of Porcellum: too bad they went to correct the good parts and kept the bad ones. Let's see what the modification consists of. The fake concept has been introduced that you have to reach 40% to trigger the majority prize. How beautiful, we have made the prize reasonable: a pity that already from 40 to 55 percent is not that reasonable. The problem is that the prize no longer goes to the coalition but to the individual party, and, knowing that no one will take 40% as a single political force, it is clear that everything will settle down through the famous ballot. By the way, do you know where the term "balleting" comes from? From the Florentine "ballotta", synonymous with chestnut. Randomly there is a tower in Florence - all the evils come from Tuscany in this period! [[Applause from the LN-Aut Group and the Senator Fattori) -]] where the priors of the arts met to make decisions and put the chestnuts inside bags; then counted the number of the chestnuts. Even this time they gave us a chestnut on the head with this ballot! I say this because the balloting is not a thing so much to be done like this. In France, those who exceed the 50% threshold get seats if they have achieved at least 25% of the votes of those entitled. On the other hand, those who go to the ballot must have achieved at least 12.5 percent of the votes of those entitled. In Italy the first two go to the ballot, even if it is a party of majority or relative minority, without a minimum threshold. Let me give an example by referring to the elections that took place in the Emilia-Romagna Region in 2014: participants in the vote of 37.71 percent (I think the votes were 34 percent); the winner won with 49.1 percent of the votes. If we round up to 50 percent (we also increase the number of votes obtained) and divide by two the percentage of 37-34 percent we see that the winner won with 17-18 percent of the votes of those entitled. The same situation can also be seen at national level where, if there is a fragmentation of the individual parties that appear autonomously, a party that gets 20% of the votes would see 340 seats allocated to the House instead of 126, thus practically tripling the electoral result. This is frankly hallucinating, but you did it. It is clear that the path of electoral law has not yet been completed. We are the only country with electoral measures to have a delayed outbreak and in fact the electoral law will enter into force in July 2016. Here, however, there is the problem of the dangerous crossroads with constitutional reform. If the constitutional reform were to receive - which I do not hope - the necessary votes in Parliament and then be confirmed in the referendum, in the next two months there would be the intervention of the Constitutional Court which, at the request of 25% of the members of the Chamber of Deputies or 30% of the members of the Senate, will have to assess the electoral law. If the parameters used by the Constitutional Court were to be the same as those used in the evaluation of the so-called Porcellum, I remind you that this electoral law has even more vices than the Porcellum. However, it makes me think that, after countless empty votes, it was possible, all of a sudden, to appoint three judges of the Constitutional Court, who may look more lovingly at Italicum and the measure will thus be able to pass the examination of the Constitutional Court. However, I ask myself the following question. If italicum were to be rejected by the Constitutional Court, we would find ourselves in a basically monocameral system with a proportional electoral system. On the contrary, if constitutional reform were to fail, the electoral law would not be subject to the scrutiny of the Constitutional Court and we would thus be in an equal bicameral system with an ultra-major electoral system in the House and absolutely proportional to the Senate. Looks to me like you guys really opened Pandora's box. Nor has the cross-section of the content of the two measures been studied here. We have already talked about this on the previous occasion, but I would like to remind you of the matter once again, so that it will remain in the proceedings of the work that led to the creation of this monster. In fact, the territorial autonomy has been abolished from the regions, depriving them of legislative power and abolishing the financial autonomy of local authorities, which no longer decide anything and can only decide whether to do or not to do on the basis of what is given to them and since they have become the collectors on behalf of the State. Let us think of the experience of the so-called Delrio law, which in fact provided for the abolition of the Provinces, which, on the other hand, still exist and cost while not really serving anything anymore. Now let's make this measure constitutional. To the fury of beating the head, even the donkey sometimes moves; you do not, go straight ahead. You've touched the Senate, good. You've reduced the number of senators. That's great. But why bring to a hundred senators and not reduce the number of House representatives by a third? [[Applause from the LN-Aut Group]] The Senate, despite what anyone who wants to see what isn't there, is a Senate made up of regional councillors who elect from among themselves. Don't tell me the dance that this happens in accordance with the popular will, because if that electoral law is not transposed by the individual regions, the regional councils will choose the senators between the regional councillors and the mayors. We don't make fun of people anymore, because that's the way it is. As it is designed, the Senate is born with a certain political plaque. The Senate will have, as its sole function, an interditive power with respect to the reform of the Constitution; except that the expression of the opinion, the only thing that can do is that, or decide that the Constitution can be modified: therefore, there is only a left majority in the House and, of course, at the level of the Senate. But having not changed the number of Members creates a huge problem from the point of view of the weights and counterweights of the system. In fact, we have shown that a minority party of 20% can obtain a majority of MEPs, with the mockery of hearing that preferences have been reintroduced. But what preferences have been reinstated? In that Parliament, the only ones who will be elected with preference (and perhaps some will) will be the 240 of the majority, that is, the party that won the ballot. Everyone else will only catch and fish from the 100 blocked capillaries. However, the elected with the preferences are not even 240: 100 are in fact the leaders of the majority party and 240 will have a true leader who will draw from there his preferences; well, they have also been able to insert the prediction that every candidate can run for 10 different colleges, so he, in turn, will decide in which college to be elected, and there is not even written that he must choose where he has less preferences, getting elected there. For example, Mr Candiani has received 30,000 preferences and Mr Arrigoni has received 1,000 of them; but if I decide to be elected to the college of Candiani as the leader, he, with 30,000 preferences, stays at home and Arrigoni, with 1,000 preferences, is elected. [[Applause from Senator Castaldi.]] Therefore, we have a party secretary who chooses Members and can blackmail them with the instrument of trust. He will therefore have the President of the Council, the President of the House and probably also the President of the Senate. But the most serious aspect concerns the guarantee bodies, which are elected in a single chamber and Senate: not changing the number of members of the Chamber, the 100 members of the Senate are absolutely and completely irrelevant. Therefore, the President of the Republic, who should be the exponent of the highest third-party, will also be a President of the Republic of... [[Brusio.]] Senator Di Maggio, if you're okay, I can talk too. You have concealed the desire not to have a third party, because you have introduced the principle that the President must be elected by two thirds of the votes. By the way, this could take several years before having a President of the Republic, because no one guarantees that two thirds will be achieved. This Senate did not deserve to be left to a political class that is not up to it - at least I think it is - and I hope it does not - to be able to stand up to everything that preceded it. Despite the various changing changes in history, this is the Senate of Rome, the Senate of the Kingdom of Italy, the Senate of the Italian Republic. Now it will become not the Senate of Italy for the Regions, but the Senate of the Regions. It will be fed to a political class and institutions that are the worst that have proved themselves in recent decades. Today this country dies of institutional costs, of public spending that cannot be reduced, and also of poor quality of public spending, and these evils are nested mainly in the regions of Italy. If this country today returns hundreds of millions of euros to the European Union because it does not know how to spend them, this is mainly due to the regions of Italy. And we deliver this institution, which I do not want to call tragically sacred but certainly important and noble, to the worst political class that currently governs this country, with a perspective, which of course is that of the immediate dissolution of the Senate. I want to say this and I repeat it to my colleagues, who think that by voting on this reform they will reach the end of the parliamentary term: it does not exist. And I think that the first to reflect on this will be her, Mr President, when she is presiding over an institution that is no longer covered by the Constitution. What will be your intimate reflection when you are at the head of an institution that our Constitution no longer provides for in its essence, in its powers and in the performance of its tasks? If, after possible approval through the referendum - which I am convinced the Italians will use to reject this absurd bill, why, in the end, the people are increasingly wiser than those who represent them - and therefore before the final ratification of this bill, the Senate of the Republic still existing, in limine mortis, should for example hesitate a motion of distrust, since the Constitution will no longer foresee that the Senate can distrust the Government, what will you do, Mr President? Will you allow the debate on a motion of distrust to carry out a Senate task no longer provided for in the Constitution? It would certainly open at least a strong constitutional dispute in this regard. And so for so many other things: for the formation of laws that will no longer be the responsibility of the Senate, will we continue to do so even if it is no longer provided for in the Constitution? I think, ladies and gentlemen, that the direct consequence of the eventual definitive approval and consecration of this rule can only be the immediate dissolution of this House at least. And so it will end, I repeat, in a very glorious way the history of a legislature in which it occurs in an absolutely unusual way, and I would like to say unexpectedly, that the senators themselves decide on the end of the institution they represent. In fact, it is here that we decide: we know very well that everything in the Chamber will be absolutely smooth, not only because of the composition in numbers, but also because of the sort of institutional conflict to which, in twenty years of presence in this Chamber, I have always witnessed, which distinguishes the relations between the Chamber and the Senate. So colleagues, it's all in your determinations, in the determinations of the majority of our colleagues. We must not turn the responsibility of this rule over to anyone, but we must give it to those who vote for it. There are, among other things, clear conditions, in these last few days, to obtain the vote also of those who perhaps wanted to carry out a reflection: the Calendar, Mr President, about which you well know how the decision to organize it has matured in such a way that this vote takes place before the renewal of the Commissions - ephemeral, because within a year these Commissions will no longer hesitate, since you will not be able to do without sending home the entire Senate, and therefore there will be no more their summits - and the examination of other draft laws that could create problems within the majority and therefore have repercussions on the vote on the reforms. These acts were postponed, because this reform had to have priority. But the acceleration, as I said, is not only the result of a parliamentary tactic, it is also a demonstration of a precise will to get rid of this Senate, where sometimes the numbers can also be not favorable to the majority and therefore can give surprises to those who instead want this country to be guided, managed and governed without any possibility of contradictory. That is the fundamental reason why we are moving forward hastily. Indeed, the House of Deputies is studying the system to speed up the constitutional procedures even more, forcing the times, where the forcings made in recent months at the regulatory and constitutional level were never enough. One last remark I would like to make to the colleagues elected in Sicily. Sicily is an autonomous region with a special status, which is once again mortified by this majority - reflected, dear colleagues of Sicily, also on this passage - in the contents of this norm. Sicily, with its five million inhabitants, with its specialty, which is the oldest in Italy, which dates back to 1946, will have seven representatives in the future Senate of the Regions; not for the Regions but for the Regions, and, please, perhaps it would be appropriate, with a next legging, to delete the word "Senato" and to bring back the future assembly to its nature of a Constitutionalized State-Regions Conference: it will certainly not be the Senate as we have understood it and how we are used to studying it also in history books. Sicily will have seven representatives with five million inhabitants; Trentino-Alto Adige will have four, with 1.5 million inhabitants. A disparity that also collides with the provisions of Article 3 of the Constitution; a disparity that arises from the need to hold firm the vote of the Group of Autonomouss because it is decisive for the approval of this reform. Once again a rule built only on the opportunity and the outcome of being able to approve it, and not in the interest of the country. That is why I returned to the first concept that I had when the debate on constitutional reform began. The Constitution, modified in its entirety and in the vast majority of its contents, as this law wants to do, is changed through a constituent Assembly and not through a Parliament that must govern everyday life with conditioning, political blackmail, with dout des and a whole series of issues that end up altering the goodness of the norm, as happened in this circumstance. If we had had a constituent assembly elected in a proportional and non-majority way (because there is also a lack of origin on the legitimacy to amend the Constitution in such a vast way by a Parliament elected by majority vote), if it had been a constituent Assembly to launch this reform, the results would have been absolutely different, much more balanced, much more respectful of the individual territorial representations (as in the case of Sicily, on which I invite Sicilian colleagues to reflect adequately); certainly much more attentive to the so-called burdens and counterweights that have always balanced the life of the Italian Republic after the approval of the Constitution of 1946. And do not call into question the constituent Fathers, because they may also be disturbed in their proper rest. [[Applause from the FI-PdL Group XVII]]

To us senators, for Renzi close to the demolition, a handful of hours was shamefully granted, between the Commission and the Assembly, to salute the bestowal to the macero of the Republican Constitution approved by the constituent Assembly on December 22, 1947 and entered into force on January 1, 1948 (so less than seventy years ago). The Senate had its functions, as did the Regions, even though they were diminished. There was a reference to standard costs, and therefore to fiscal federalism; there was a link between the State and the European Union. Everything's been canceled. It is unacceptable for the League to return to the state centralization of any major choice, strengthened even with the supremacy clause whereby the State is given the opportunity to intervene in all matters of competence of the Regions thus making disappear any form of autonomy. The abolition of federalism at variable speeds is unacceptable. It is unacceptable to relegate the Senate to an empty place of functions without autonomous legislative activity and therefore to little more than a post-rail job, without any significant role for future senators and senator mayors. It is unacceptable that senators are not directly elected and will therefore be deprived of any direct control by the people. In fact, the provision that senators should be identified in accordance with the choices made by voters at the time of the renewal of the same regional councils is a mockery, it is a deception for citizens! The regional councillors will elect from among themselves. All bad provisions, which should have led to prefer a more dignified choice for the Senate itself, i.e. its suppression. The North League would have wanted a different measure: with more federalism, with more popular sovereignty, with less interference than the European Union and less public spending. Well, in the months we are living there is a clear political will, which is expressed through the vote in this House, the vote in the parliamentary classrooms. And this is the important fact that needs to be emphasized. There are no goodsmen, there are no constraints. I believe that we are all free people and today we have taken the responsibility to carry out a period of reforms that the country has been waiting for twenty years and which has led - I believe also as a Democratic Party - to a great deal of confrontation within us. We are modernising the country, which - watch out - is an action that can only happen where there is a mature democracy. This is the first fundamental aspect. A mature democracy manages to grasp the limits of its own structuring, even institutional, and the evolutions of society. It is a democracy that manages to grasp how our country can remain competitive in a much more complex international framework and that decides to take initiatives of important reforms, which go to change the country deeply, but they go to make it more responsive to the needs of citizens and businesses and - I believe - overall more responsive to the need to represent the country within the parliamentary classrooms. This is the path we have taken, which has strongly involved and also crossed the Democratic Party. The PD had to modernise itself in order to be able to be the driving force behind these reforms, and this does not happen simply or easily, but requires an application, a capacity for reflection and confrontation that we have been able to put in place not only within the party - and I do not even remember all the steering meetings that we have done to discuss the various points - but also here in Parliament. We started from different positions and we reached an important agreement, a significant meeting point between positions that found the conditions to make a choice. And when a choice in Parliament sees the United Democratic Party, it is also able to attract the responsibilities and capabilities of other political forces, who know how to lead back to important choices for the country. In the minutes I have available, I want to remain politically alone. I think we're in front of a watershed. After all the last years of discussions on reforms, getting to the bottom of them creates a situation of watersheds: what it was before will not be after and we are going to live a future as protagonists, and this applies to everyone. It is a watershed for Italian politics, because with this ability to implement reforms we give credibility to the policy that, in the surveys and measurements carried out, stands at 5% or at very low percentages of liking. By showing that we are also able to influence the liturgies and conditions of politics that today sees in so many citizens an attitude of distrust, we try to demonstrate that, before we even get our hands on the things that affect the country, we started from ourselves, and I believe that this is important. But then, when we did this, we went to put in place an important reform action also in the country. The reforms that we have put in place in recent months and which the Government has taken forward have, of course, all been mentioned in the negative sense. It was a blocked country that lived on its own certainties and, unarmed, looked at their loss without moving a finger. And we have tried to deepen dynamism, to gather the best energies, to motivate young people and give back strength to the engine that is at the base of a country, which is the school. We have tried to free energy through initiatives such as jobs act. For what I have seen in my story experience on the territory of what we are doing, I believe there are growing awareness and satisfaction of the work done. It was difficult to imagine that Parliament could do all this, but instead we recovered the conditions for sharing a political will capable of deciding and not doing what has always been done, namely postponing it. In the face of the problems we have not been able to find a solution and so we have postponed, so another will think about it, we will think about it tomorrow or the day after tomorrow. No: let's think about it now and face up to the issues, take our responsibilities and modernise the country, with great confidence in the citizens. Doing what we're doing is basically monocameralism. When one hears that, rather than that, it was necessary to close the Senate, it is a joke that no longer believes even those who pronounce it. We have tried to give a real answer to the country, which is a substantial mono-cameralism, but it takes into account its institutional structure, made up of local autonomy, regions and municipalities, and raises it to the equal rank of Parliament in order to contribute to its development. One cannot but understand this depth, this reflection, something that really goes in the direction of better understanding the reality in which we live. Sometimes I think that I have colleagues who have their eyes closed in front of reality, because ours is a country that has made a way, that has been able to experience also a federalism that today, with this reform, we resume and rewrite, through a new relationship between State and Regions. Here we give a judgment on the past years. We recognize that something has not worked, that on some issues specific to the concurrent legislation between State and Regions you cannot continue to go on in this way; that there is the need to make our Country competitive, that passes through an ability to formulate strategies on important issues that must be carried out through the Government and the central State, with the contribution and consultation of all other institutional levels, but through this taking of responsibility to make strategic choices. PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. Senator, I invite you to conclude. COLLINA (PD). Madam President, in the time I had available, I tried to cut this down on my speech, because I felt it was inappropriate to list all the issues contained in the reform - which we know very well - which, of course, have all been mentioned in a negative way. I believe that this last passage to the Senate - and I conclude, President - is not a diversion. And we do this not to distract someone's attention, but to continue to keep our citizens' attention on these issues and on all the social formations present in our country, which I believe will respond positively. The referendum is not just a matter of citizens and parties; it is also a matter of a living, organized and structured country which, in my opinion, has taken full advantage of the commitment we have made and which we have maintained. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u51	He's enlisted to speak Senator Quagliariello. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u52	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen of the government, this text concerns Title V and bicameralism: an important part of that great reform that was in the aspirations with which the legislature opened, which also included the revision of the form of government and, in harmony with the new architecture, the modification of the electoral law. In short, today we are talking about a partial measure, but that does not mean that, if approved, this reform will still have historical value. At the previous reading I voted in favour, and I can say that some of this reform belongs to me. More importantly, it belongs to that Committee of Experts which I had the honour of chairing as Minister of Reform, which has worked intensively for months in a spirit of cooperation and on which the current Premier has not failed to exercise his irony at the proper time, asking - I quote - to retire the essays who retire to Francavilla. The result of that work was finally imposed on the extravagant proposal that had been made and that provided for a Senate made up of mayors and appointed by the President of the Republic, and therefore, because of its composition, unable to play that role as compensation chamber between regional legislator and national legislator able to return to Parliament a bit of that sovereignty that the permanent dispute between State and Regions devolved to the Constitutional Court. As far as this text is concerned, I consider this to be a good thing. In order to assess the disruptions, we need to widen the view. Each reform must be judged in a more comprehensive institutional context. (17.28) (Followed by Mr Quagliarello). Each part must take account of the others. And the context in which this reform falls partly conditioning it. We have abolished the relationship of trust between the Government and a Chamber and have laid the foundations for the Senate to enter only a few, very few great political forces. We have, therefore, reduced representation. This should have been taken into account by the electoral law that, instead, challenging the "spirit of time" and without ensuring stability (that stability that does not depend on an electoral law and a bi-party format, as the experience of the People of Freedom taught us, and that much more depends on those institutional mechanisms that we await from the famous agenda Perassi), forces the institutional orthopedia to impose on the system a bi-party format. And our time is not even a time of bipolarism: it is not in Italy, but not in France or Spain. And electoral laws are always empirical and approximate instruments that political time must take into account. Not surprisingly, fathers of the proportional reform of 1919, such as Don Luigi Sturzo and Salvemini, at a different juncture became supporters of majority systems. In short: in a context in which the space of representation is already compressed, and in which throughout Europe the right-left fault is no longer the exclusive ground of political confrontation, we have approved an electoral law that effectively abolishes the coalitions, forces the current government allies to fade their identity and relegates them to the prospect of being allied until the day before the vote, and then have to choose between loneliness and absorption in the lists of the main party (I do not want to talk about affiliation). We did so with a vote of confidence that saw outside the parliamentary assemblies not only oppositions, but even a part of the relative majority party. This situation, which was later claimed as a "parliamentary masterpiece." Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I personally am among those, and not from today, who have felt that Italian democracy needed more efficiency rather than more counterweights. I am well aware that the Commission's structural weakness in the Constitutional Charter of 1947 stems from peculiar historical circumstances: from the veil of ignorance which at the time prevented us from knowing which of the two sides that referred to the dominating superpowers of the Cold War would prevail; from the border position between the two blocks in which geopolitics forced our country, even more after the break between Stalin and Tito; finally, from the consociative practice (I use the term in a technical and not derogatory sense) that dominated the material development of our political life after 1953 for the presence in Italy of the largest communist party in the Western world. In short, I am not afraid of authoritarian torsions and I do not share, with respect to the text in our examination, alarms in this regard. I did not even share the view that, in the revision of the bicameral structure, the second Chamber should become an institutional counterweight, because it is not in any part of the world. Nevertheless, the problem of a constitutional balance also exists for countries, such as Italy, in which the executive power has suffered from a congenital weakness and its strengthening represents a healthy therapy. And this problem must be considered, by identifying the counterweights in other gears of the institutional machine. During the process of examining the measure in the Senate I had indicated some of them: the rationalization of the Authorities and effective parliamentary control; the forecast of a Joint Committee on Public Finance - God knows how much we would need it today - presided over by a member of the oppositions as core business of their statutes. I have to say that not only have those amendments not been accepted, but the problem of constitutional imbalance has worsened at the last reading, providing for a method of election of the President of the Republic that potentially leads him back to the dynamics of the relative majority party: too little for him to be considered a guarantor, enough for an internal minority of that party to be able, in hypothesis, to block the election and, with it, to paralyze the system. It is with constructive spirit and without any conservative intent that I have therefore presented five draft laws that specifically concern the reform of the electoral law, the statute of parties, the election of the President of the Republic, the Authorities and the statute of oppositions. If taken into account, they could prevent this reform from being unfinished and eventually a missed opportunity. I am well aware that not everything can be done right away, and I must also acknowledge that the interlocking of my concerns, even more than my own, by Minister Boschi did not stop at the time of the last transition of the reform to the Senate. This recognition of the Minister's work and courtesy cannot, however, fail to note that so far no political commitment has been made, if not one of the opposite sign: that of not changing the electoral law, in spite also of the alleged - very supposed - private reassurances announced by the government allies. Finally, Mr President, this reform is part of a political framework that cannot be ignored. At least it cannot be ignored by those who had begun this legislature in the sign of an ancient hope: the hope that the great political cultures that have deep roots in the history of this country could finally recognize and legitimize themselves. The result of the urns of 2013 gave us in fact a historic opportunity: the end of a bipolarism rissoso; the possibility that the rules of a new season of the Republic were written together, also to give answers to an electorate that by giving a huge consensus (it is right who preceded me) to the 5 Star Movement had sounded loud alarm bell. I defended that hope in every possible way. I did so by not pulling back from strong choices when a government that was then really emergency would have been demolished, without having an alternative design. I did this by saying goodbye to the Nazarene pact as a possible recovery time. I also defended that hope by leaving an institutional post to contribute more discreetly, but equally effective, to the implementation of the reform. In more than half the legislature I cannot help but take note of what is left of that hope. It governs with variable geometry majorities, depending on themes, circumstances and conveniences. The possibility, not only of an emerging collaboration, but also of a coalition relationship, has in fact been cleared. Finally, there is a worrying centralisation of power: the reform of the public television service raises no less perplexity than what the European Union has expressed in relation to Polish law, and press reports draw equally reassuring scenarios on issues such as municipal control or cybersecurity. It is consistent with such a context, but it is not acceptable, and much less acceptable, to conceive of constitutional reform as a ground for ultimate confrontation; to brandish it as a weapon against conservatisms that certainly exist, to overcome, however, not the resistance to modernization, Mr Collina, but political concerns of a very different nature. I am well aware, Mr President, that a political reading of the referendum would eventually come. But launching the referendum campaign when we are still debating in these classrooms, and legitimizing the premature and instrumental constitution of opposing committees is another political error in which the President-in-Office of the Council has incurred. This does not create a new bipolarism, but at the most we stage a clash between system and antisystem that not only does not represent a advancement, but is a regression towards a blocked system that Italy has known for long decades and that today finds no justification in the international context. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, we left almost three years ago trying to unite the country in the sign of a necessary reform. We have linked legal and constitutional cultures which had been opposed for decades and which have proved themselves willing to cooperate. We have come to the end of this affair with a reform that divides the country and that brings conservatism into play because this is instrumentally useful to an all-political result. This risks transforming a historic opportunity into a new ride. It may last long or short, but in the end the country will be at its starting point. It is to the outcome of this parable and in the light of these growing fears, of which I have left constant trace in the parliamentary acts, that I have taken the decision, suffering, not to participate in the vote, knowing well to abandon without denying it a part of the work that has seen me protagonist, and giving this choice a sense that is not of despair, but of proposal, hope and struggle. So that something can still change by recovering the furrow from which we started. [[Applause from the LAG Group (GS, PPI, M, MBI, Id, E-E)]]

ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u54	Mr President, I am sorry to have listened to Mr Quagliariello's conclusions, because I believe that the fact that he believes that he cannot vote for the further reading of the reform, of which he has been a member, if not a large part, is something that is not pleasing. But this attitude and these words that we have just heard presuppose a background. There are those who, like me, have made the reverse path and have explained, of course in general indifference, that it was no longer a question of sharing some aspects of constitutional reform, but of going to support the reasons for a politicization of this battle, which sincerely affects little or nothing to the goodness of the provision. I am not Carneade who, contrary to what Manzoni wrote, was rather well known to be the head of a school, that of distichous, philosophers so good that they were able to support the thesis and antithesis with equal value. So much so that when the good Carneade went to Rome to support both the thesis and the antithesis if Greece had to pay tributes to Rome, Cato, an expression of Roman concreteness, took it by the ear and kicked it out saying that in Rome the yes is yes and the no is no. I am not tender in assessing this reform. In my view, this kind of reform was a necessity. The sovereign determination of the indication of one of the Chambers is taken away from the people, because it seemed to me to read that the Senate is not suppressed, but that its competences are changed, and in some way the choice by the voters has been recovered; although I confess that I did not understand well what the mechanism through which citizens will indicate the 94 senators who will come here to settle the issues relating to the different competences between State and Regions. I do not share the fact that we wanted to pursue the volcano according to which, in a state with 2,200 billion euros of state debt, which still has 8,000 to 9,000 participants (which produce 45 billion euros of debt) and which has a budget that for two thirds consists of pensions, salaries and health care and for the other third from interest on the public debt, we have passed as a signal of reclamation of the politics and resistence of the political world to have abolished the salaries of 315 senators. But, apart from this aspect (everything in Italy becomes instrumental), the Renzi Government game is played on this reform. If Renzi had gone under in one of the secret ballots, as well as from the wishes of that belligerent part that he in turn ordained plots and pitfalls towards his own party secretary, as well as the President of the Council (unless we find that the cause of their misunderstandings is Denis Verdini and the AL-A Group, when this comes from a completely internal dichotomy between a President of the Council who has a more liberal vision of society and the state and old war remnants of socialism and social democracy), I said that, if this Government had gone under, we would go to the elections. Senator Quagliariello told us that, in fact, the game in the coming months will no longer be played on the goodness of the reforms, but rather on the confirmation or not of this political order and on Renzi's permanence to the Presidency of the Council or on his expulsion not to the fury of parliamentary vote, but to the rage of the people. Therefore, despite this preeminent fact, which is a political fact that no longer pertains to constitutional architecture, the fact remains that we have abolished, between the two institutions, the oldest and perhaps noblest (I do not want Members of Parliament); the fact remains that the election mechanism is not clear and therefore the suffrage that the citizens express in their choice. And it remains a great failed battle of the center-right, to propose, as a moment of compensation, the direct election of the Head of State. In fact - we can turn around as we want - a democracy nouns giving the so-called sovereign people the opportunity to choose the rulers. When this fails, I do not say that there is someone who interprets the rules to make them authoritarian, but I say that there is a rushing idea of democracy, in which the direct element is the speed of decisions and not the goodness of the decisions themselves. So I can't deny myself, and I'm not going to deny it, where I say you could do much better than you did. However, when this becomes a matter of political confrontation, with a quite varied committee for the "no," which goes from the 5 Star Movement to Forza Italia (which is all to say), obviously there are forces contrary to the Government - I say to the Government of the reforms - and favorable forces. As far as I'm concerned, I'm politically on the side of those who want to reform this country and not those who want to keep it. So the political sense of the matter prevails in me. It is true that for over fifty years we have lost the good to pursue the good. All reforms can be improved. Even the one made by D'Onofrio and Calderoli, which we submitted to the Italian people and which failed, in my humble opinion it was extremely better than this, more organic and was better suited to our tradition and to our political history. However, the people are sovereign and sovereignty does not give the people infallibility. Therefore, a foolishness perpetrated, as Saint-Just said, in such a vast way becomes a wise thing and the great crimes, like the great wars that have produced millions of deaths, probably no longer become murders, but the battles of freedom or salvation of the people. With the same spirit in which Senator Quagliariello says that he cannot vote for what he voted, I am going to vote for what I did not vote for the first time, not because he changed his mind about what is the fabric of the reform, but because the times that parade before us are of a field choice: either you are with those who want to reform or you are with those who want to preserve. Between the two options, I can only choose change. [[Applause from the AL-A Group. Congratulations.]]
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u55	He's enlisted to speak Senator Lucidi. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u56	Mr President, colleagues and citizens who are listening to us and watching from home, I thank you for your attention. I shall make some general comments which I have already partly made in the Commission. The first question we have to ask ourselves, in my opinion, at the time when we came to the conclusion of these work on constitutional reform, as we have already done many times for the first version of the Constitution, is: whose daughter is this reform? Whose daughter will this Constitution be? That's the real question. It is then clear that this Constitution is and will be the daughter of the conflict of interest. This is the only expression that is probably still missing from our parliamentary debate. Our Constitution, downstream of this constitutional reform, will be the daughter of the conflict of interest. And here I make a suggestion to Minister Boschi for the next reforms. A reform to be carried out in Italy could probably be to eliminate the expression: "conflict of interest". If a daughter calls a father and tells him that she has been appointed Minister of the Italian Republic, the father should probably tell her that it is incorrect, it is not fair, it is not logical, it is not moral. So this is not a conflict of interest. The proposal for a forthcoming reform that I make to the Minister is to abolish this expression and to include, perhaps in the Constitution or in some measure, that of harmony of interests. That is the expression under which this Constitution will be brought to light. Unfortunately, in order to rewrite the Constitutional Charter we should have carefully analysed the needs of our country and this has not been done. We have said that this Constitution will be the daughter of the conflict of interests and - another important element - it will be the daughter of external and clearly party interests, of a fundamentally unique party, by a majority Democratic Party. Another question that we should ask ourselves, as well as how, is also: who makes these reforms and who is completing them. Very often in recent months we have wondered whether or not this Parliament was entitled to carry out these reforms. A comparison has been made with the constituent fathers like Calamandrei, to name one. This name has been resonated several times in this House. The problem is that this reform will be carried out by an Italian political leader who, in my opinion, has perpetrated one of the biggest thefts that have ever been carried out to the detriment of the citizens. In fact, one of the reasons why the 5 Star Movement is now in this House is that Italians and citizens have been taken away from a fundamental principle, namely that of delegation, on which we have based our whole society and our civilization. The principle of delegation unites the Italian people with the citizens of New York, Tokyo, with the indigenous people of the Amazon, because our whole life is linked to the principle of delegation. At this moment all our lives are delegated, but in Italy there is only one thing that is no longer possible to delegate and it is the administration of our cities and our public life. This is because of the ruling class that is reforming the Constitution and that has taken away from us one of the founding elements of our civilization, that is, the principle of delegation. That said, in fact, we of the 5 Star Movement (not with the Democratic Party, which is still a body in the making, because it must shape itself according to the changes in the times that follow), the citizens and Prime Minister Renzi we share the same problem, that is the total absence of Parliament, and not from this legislature, but for decades. A few months ago, at a previous reading, I tried to make an account of the draft laws presented in the last legislatures, going backwards until XIII, and discovered that there are about 50,000 draft laws presented and never discussed. This brings out another paradox of our political system: I am referring to the paradox or real conflict between the electoral programme and the government programme. It is true that we are currently facing a debate that was not written in a political programme, so we are in breach of the mandate given to a certain part of this branch of Parliament. Unfortunately, another thing we have learned in these palaces is that, at such a high level sometimes institutional relations are governed by a bond made of respect for roles, institutional positions and very often nothing of this is written. Well, the lobbyists, the outside interests, the tailstrokes of the political caste have the strength to intervene on these subtle bonds. Renzi is doing just that, that is to say he intervenes with a tense leg on a fragility of the system, and this Parliament has failed to oppose this, because in this House everyone in the majority has simply tried to preserve their own interests. This is also happening in these hours, because the examination of constitutional reform takes place immediately before the renewal of the Commissions; therefore, with the usual partitioning logic: simply for two years of Vice-President in the Commission the transformation and degradation of our Constitution and our country is bartered. There has been talk very often of an authoritarian drift that will take place downstream of the reform under discussion. In my opinion, however, such authoritarian drift has already been in place for a long time, because if a city like Taranto cannot breathe a healthy air to its children, probably such drift already exists; if the citizen is daily away from the place where the fate of the cities and the nation is decided, then probably the authoritarian drift already exists; if a Parliament composed of 1,000 parliamentarians can not oppose in a strong and energetic way a reform that is the faded photocopy and badly done of the same reform ten years ago, probably the authoritarian drift already exists. If a Parliament, made up of political veterans, fails to do this, then, in my opinion, the authoritarian drift is already there. We have also lived it on our own in the various committees: for example, in the Foreign Affairs Committee we have even been asked not to table amendments to a decree-law, because they would not have been adopted. This phrase, thrown there, seemed almost normal, but it means exactly that this sense of emptying and this authoritarian drift are already present, permeate already and have already been metabolised by Parliament. On the other hand, we claim the right to table amendments and to influence Parliament's readings, because then, at the end of our mandate, as it should be, we will have to account to the citizens of our work. Therefore, by not tabling amendments and pretending that we have not even read the various laws that pass through this House, we would not be able to account to the citizens for our actions. This final statement, however, will have to be done by everyone: not just by us, but also by the majority. I shall conclude my speech by simply saying that the constitutional reform that you are going to approve, probably tomorrow, and to which we will also oppose during the subsequent referendum campaign, is an illegitimate reform, because it will determine an illegitimate Constitution, as the daughter of a cancer, which surrounds the Italian institutions and envelops the political class of this country, which is constituted by the conflict of interest, from which Minister Boschi is not exempt, but, indeed, the flag bearer. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u57	Senator Bencini is registered to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u58	Mr President, Minister, ladies and gentlemen, at least in our capacity as senators, we have reached the final act of a reform process, strongly desired by the current head of the Renzi Government and the previous President of the Republic of Naples. They have the burden and the honour of being the main advocates and leaders of a reform that, if confirmed by the voters, will represent the most incisive and profound modification to the Republican Constitution of our Italian history. They must be recognized the ability to have been able to impose a constituent destiny on a legislature that certainly was not born under such auspices. The political difficulties to form a government, which followed the outcome of the vote and, above all, the decision of the Constitutional Court on the unconstitutionality of the electoral law, which defined the composition of the current Parliament, undoubtedly made it easier to predict the rapid approval of a new electoral law above any doubt of unconstitutionality and early elections. I have always recalled, at every parliamentary passage of this reform, the institutional delicacy of the situation in which we were called to express our vote and I have always asked the majority and the Government, also because of the particular nature of the context, to listen to and accept the proposals for changes of those who had no interest in boycotting the reform, instead sharing the general system, but who simply highlighted some defects and contradictions. I have tried until the last, along with so many other colleagues, to seek constructive dialogue in the general interest because I am aware that there was a wider majority in this House sincerely intending to give effect to the debate on overcoming the limits of perfect bicameralism and the inefficiencies of parliamentarism by means of a reform that was not subject to the confiscated majority of government and opposition. This approach, which today I share with Italy of the Values, has never made me adhere neither to the faction of the idolaters nor to the faction of the apocalyptic ones, nor to place among those who have always defended the text of reform, denying the existence of criticalities even where they appeared evident, nor among those who claim that such a reformer plan is a threat to democracy. I continue to believe that a more shared and lighter reform text could and should have been presented to citizens than some easily eliminated defects. We should have found the courage to do more, for example to reduce the number of Members as well in order to make the relationship between the two Houses more balanced and to further cut the costs of politics. We could have done more from the point of view of participatory and direct democracy, inserting instruments that are present in other European countries and correcting, as well as Title V, also the rule on balance of budget, as one could and should have included among the bicameral subjects equal national electoral law. But despite all the right considerations we can make about regrets and missed opportunities in the process of this law, it remains truly incomprehensible to me how technically it can be argued that the present reform represents, as has also been said by authoritative scholars, the transition from democracy to oligarchy or that it represents a threat to parliamentary democracy. I do not believe that such a negative judgment is consistent with a comparative approach. Constitution in force at hand we have two chambers that vote for the government. Both the House and the Senate therefore have a majority that supports the Premier and that binds to the fate of the government. The President of the Republic is elected after the third ballot with the quorum of the absolute majority of the members of an Assembly in which only regional delegates can take away from the majority of Government the power to elect the President autonomously. The judges of the Constitutional Court shall be elected by the Parliament in a joint sitting where the Government has a majority on its part. Instead, an independent Senate that does not vote for the government's confidence, which gradually renews itself in its components and which has on some matters, including the revision of the Constitution, the same legislative power as the Lower House, is a counterweight to the power of the Premier far superior to the current Upper House. With the new Senate it will be much more difficult for a Premier to impose his constitutional reform. The reform in the vote does not reduce the guarantee bodies provided for in the current Constitution and does not increase the power of appointment and therefore control that the majority of Government holds over them. It can be said, indeed, that the reformed Constitution appears to guarantee oppositions to a greater extent than the current Charter does. Just think of the election of the President of the Republic or the election of the judges of the Constitutional Court, guarantee figures of our legal system: the reform provides for the minimum quorum of the three fifths of the voters, a threshold greater than the current text. We have been very displeased about the part relating to this rule because the possibility remained open, if there is no real sharing on the President of the Republic, of not being able to elect it because the numbers would always be lacking if the oppositions were always present in the Chamber during the elections. In other Western democracies, the Premier has the power to revoke ministers, or can dissolve the Chambers, or is protected by the institute of constructive distrust. None of this has been introduced. The powers of the Premier have not been increased and if the future President of the Council can count on a solid and faithful majority this will not depend on the constitutional reform under discussion, but rather on the hyper-majoritarian electoral law that has been approved. You, President Calderoli, have already well argued limits and inconsistencies of such electoral law; you have also made a comparison with previous laws and a comparison with the electoral systems of other countries, so you have well explained what this electoral law could possibly cause in conjunction with constitutional reform. In fact, however, we are talking about constitutional reform, so we must act here and now excluding electoral reform because, as we know, electoral law is an ordinary law, it is not a higher-ranking law and therefore it can be changed when and how it is desired by any majority that will come in the near future and in any case even in this legislature could undergo changes even in the light of any unconstitutionality that the Constitutional Court might raise on it. However, as I have already said, a constitutional reform cannot be assessed on the basis of an ordinary law, however important it may be. If the reform is approved, the Court - at the request of Members - will assess the electoral law and see if it is deemed unconstitutional. But, in any case, the current text protects minorities worse than the new Charter that we are going to vote on can do. Ultimately, if it is true that the excellent is the enemy of good, this reform is not the excellent but the good, where it responds to a parliamentary debate started with the Commission Bozzi of 1983 and which over the last thirty years has produced in the political and academic context the awareness of the need to end the experience, almost unique in the scenario of the present democracies, of the perfect bicameralism. It is good because the long transition between the first and the second Republic finally ends, giving the country an institutional system suitable for a democracy of alternation. It is good because it does this without changing the form of government in our country, with prudence, in line with the choice of the constituent Fathers. It is good because it knows how to take note, as for Title V, that by reforming you can also make mistakes but that you can evaluate a reform only if it is tried. Therefore, we in Italy of the Values are convinced that a Constitution is alive if we also have the courage to reform it. We are convinced that the present generation has the right to be called upon to contribute to a renewed Charter of Fundamental Rights. And if this reform isn't the best, it's good. We want the citizens to be able to speak through a referendum on this text, to be informed and that the debate does not turn into a plebiscite on the Prime Minister. Instead, we want to invite voters to fully understand the importance of their choice. And we will do so with the intellectual awareness and honesty of those who recognize the merits and limits of reform and will not make electoral propaganda about our Constitution. [[Applause from Senator Molinari]]

The law will be subject to the scrutiny of the citizens since I doubt, I do not think so, and I can foresee that there will not be a two-thirds majority tomorrow. I trust that information will do its duty, which is to inform Italian citizens and citizens of what is happening so that everyone is informed of the facts. Then the Italian citizens will decide. If the Italian citizens approve this reform, it means that the reform will be democratically recognised and will become operational and if they do not vote for it - and I hope they do not vote for it - everyone will assume their responsibilities and Parliament will start working again, because the law governing democracy is to respect the will of the people, to adapt to it and to behave consistently. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u66	Mr President, this 2016 will certainly be an important year for the history of our country. Here it is not the victory of a political force or the prestige of a President of the Council, but the very estate of our Republic. This stability in Italy was not there because the party system did not allow it. And then it is the system of parties that, if anything, must be modified, not already the Constitution, which certainly did not want it as it is now and cannot therefore be held responsible for it. And this, ladies and gentlemen of the government, is the boulder that remains at the end of this operation! [[Applause from Senator Mussini.]]

One day, in a beautiful country called Italy, a king, King George, who lived in Palazzo del Quirinale, decided that it was time to follow the advice of JP Morgan, that is, to put hand to Italian democracy. In fact, the giant responsible for the US economic crisis had expressed himself as follows: The political and constitutional systems of the South have the following characteristics: Weak executives vis-à-vis Parliaments; Weak central governments vis-à-vis the regions; constitutional protection of workers' rights. The political systems of the countries of the South, and in particular their Constitutions, adopted following the fall of fascism, have a number of characteristics that seem unsuitable to favour the greater integration of the European area. Who knows what integration he was talking about? But let's go back to this modern fairy tale. Together with some friends, including Mr. Mario Draghi, current President of the European Central Bank, with whom he fraternally met at Bar Three Eyes, convinced that democracy could hinder their plans for a new world order based on banking and financial interests, King George decided to disband the President of the Council in office and crown another. Not just anyone... needed someone who could continue their work. They then organized, all very happy and enthusiastic about the project to be concluded, the new coronation. The chosen was Mr. Mario Monti, also very friendly with those strong powers that in Europe make good and bad weather and therefore willing to listen and carry out the project. To insert it into the Italian political landscape, however, it needed a clever move. "How can this be done?" asked the king. That's when King George had enlightenment. He would have appointed him senator for life for his merits (future or past is not given to know) and then he would have entrusted him with the constitution of the new Government of the Italian Republic. Finally the deal was made, the project could go on. Mr Monti put himself at work and first destroyed Italy's economic autonomy, including the balance of the budget in the Constitution. A first step towards the new world order had been taken. Now, however, democracy, the power of the citizens, had to be weakened. "What could we do?," asked the friends of the Three Eyes bar. What can lead a citizen to lower his head in front of his or her rights? The hunger, the hunger, screamed Mr. Monti from the rear. Here's the solution: he needed to starve the people. Mr Monti, then, completed the Fornero reform: raising the retirement age (i.e. less generational turnover resulting in less work for young people) and hunger pensions. The game was done. But time was running and, despite all the efforts made, the plan for the new world order was not over. The time had expired, the elections were looming and democracy in Italy had not yet been defeated. Friends at Three Eyes' bar consulted each other and after various discussions they decided to rely on the case, not before giving him directions on the right path. The elections for the renewal of Parliament were stimulant on the one hand, but worrying on the other. A new political force had emerged, the 5 Star Movement, made up of citizens from all over Italy to enter the Palaces and defeat injustices. No one knew them, they were all censored and with their hands free. The Three Eyes coven began, therefore, to wonder: "How can we stop these free and non-blackmailable citizens?" Another enlightening idea of King George: "They must remain unknown, no one must speak of it, they must not exist for anyone! No publicity about their existence, as if they were extraterrestrial! The day of the election came, and, despite all the obstacles, the most voted symbol was precisely the 5 Star Movement. The strategies implemented by King George's group had not been effective, the citizen had won. Enrico had worked hard for a year, but he didn't have that grit, the desire to appear, the desire to command that he needed at that moment. We needed someone smarter, more crazy. Someone willing to do anything to look like King George and override him in the business rooms. It was precisely during those months that Matteo Renzi, who had been wandering around institutions in search of power, had come to Rome as the leader of the Democratic Party. The two bridesmaids also offered guarantees: they frequented the same environments as King George, especially Miss Boschi, who, with her family, frequented businessmen and bankers. Safer than that!
