ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u73	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I will begin my speech in this House by saying that constitutional reform should represent a substantial change in the rules of democracy. And it's not random to use parole, because that's not how it's happening. The text of the Constitutional Reform, which has arrived in the Senate armoured by the majority, is once again an expression of the Democratic Party's will not to dialogue with the opposition, to rise above the parties and to decide the fate of the Republic at will. To amend the Constitution is an event in which all political forces must have the opportunity to participate, a moment where ideas and aggregation meet, where dialogue between the interlocutors gives positive results and does not turn into a political conflict. In the beginning Forza Italia decided to sit at the table of the constitutional reform process, having in its heart the most sincere seriousness to change the rules for the good of the country. He decided to cooperate, to seek everyone's cooperation for the success of the reforms. The Constitution, as we know, is the basic written legislative act, which defines the nature and discipline of the organization of the supreme state organs and the proclamation of a series of rights and duties in which a people is recognized. The main arguments contained in the constitutional reform, which concern the change of the Senate and consequently the fate of each senator, are: the nature of the Senate (Article 1, amending Article 55 of the Constitution), the composition of the Senate (Article 2, amending Article 57 of the Constitution) and its legislative functions (Article 10, amending Article 70 of the Constitution). The changes made to Articles 55 and 70 of the Constitution have diminished, with the changes made by the House, their legislative functions and not only. In fact, Article 1 of the Constitutional Bill, which amends Article 55, provides for a strong revision of the functions proper to the two branches of Parliament: differentiated bicameralism. Article 10, which replaces Article 70 of the Constitution, differentiates the powers that Chamber and Senate can exercise in the formation of laws. Two types of procedure are defined, bicameral and monocameral; there will also be a specific monocameral procedure with a reinforced role of the Senate for those only laws that will apply the supremacy clause (Article 31, which amends Article 117 of the Constitution). With regard to Article 2, there is a lack of legitimacy for the way in which individual senators are elected: 95 senators elected by regional councils and autonomous provinces, plus 5 senators appointed by the President of the Republic. The number of elective senators decreases significantly, from 315 to only 95; in addition, direct popular voting is replaced by a second grade election. It is necessary to return to a direct election of the senators; it is evident that Article 1 of the Constitution is violated in relation to the election of the second degree of the Senate, where the people, the holder of sovereignty, have the right to exercise their vote in favour of the chosen candidate. The new amended text shows a clear difference between the two Chambers in numerical terms: 100 senators versus 630 Members, who will have a greater weight in the possible choice of judges of the Constitutional Court. In fact, the first paragraph of Article 37, which amends Article 135 of the Constitution, intervenes in the matter of the election of the judges of the Constitutional Court. Article 135 is reworded in order to provide for the new composition of the Constitutional Court: fifteen judges, one third of whom are appointed by the President of the Republic, one third by the supreme ordinary and administrative magistrates, three by the Chamber of Deputies and two by the Senate. A clear division since the current appointment of the five constitutional judges, who are elected by Parliament in common sitting. There is a desire on the part of the majority in government to leave the Senate in limbo, without giving it a precise task, nor an institutional lease. There will be an unelected Senate, composed exclusively of regional councillors - we know there will also be 21 mayors - where they will continue to increase the power of territorial lobbyists or corporate lobbyists. One could think of diversifying the powers between the House and the Senate in another way; one could guarantee the Senate a role as a legislative assembly with specific tasks, freed from government trust, with strong powers of control and rebalancing. In conclusion, Forza Italia believes that the Constitutional Charter must continue to be the fundamental pillar of democracy for the Italian people and the opinion on this reform is strongly opposed. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, on the site 'deputatipd.it' - just to tell you the confusion in which you yourselves navigate, colleagues in the PD - there is talk of a simpler Italy and it is said that the new Constitution is even closer and that there will be more efficiency, less costs and more powers to citizens. Then they say: find out more. When we go to read, there are some impressive inaccuracies, which PD Members should also be aware of. It is even said that regional and provincial councillors will participate in the vote. Evidently they were not sufficiently clear in saying that, when it comes to provincial councilors, reference was probably made to the two autonomous Provinces of Trento and Bolzano. Then they go on to say that the Provinces will be eliminated. There is, therefore, such a contradiction that provincial councilors should vote for when the Provinces themselves move towards elimination according to the Delrio reform. We are talking about cuts in politics and more powers for citizens. But where is this? What are the policy cuts? Let's say as long as it's a mockery of citizens. If people are to believe that the Regional Councillor is not being paid as a senator, we must also tell them that he is being paid as a Regional Councillor, that it is not a saving in the allowance because we throw away the allowance. We steal the compensation to the citizens because it will not do the Regional Councillor good, nor the senator good. And how do we think to say: "more powers for citizens" when, at some point, with the new electoral law, where the list and not the coalition is awarded, we are faced with the fact that the list with a 30% that could win will have a Bulgarian majority in Parliament, but that it is not representative of the citizens and does not respect the power of the citizens? In fact, if we consider that about 60% of the population is going to vote, we will be facing about 20% of the popular representation. Does this mean giving more powers to citizens? Dear colleagues and members of the majority, is this the reform that will have to turn our Constitution around? Alas, no. You missed a great chance. Unfortunately, we are faced with a reform that is ill-formed, reform that is not shaped and reform that is different from the real demands and the real powers that citizens should have and do not have. Ladies and gentlemen, think about this: this is not the right way. [[Applause from the FI-PdL Group XVII]]
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u74	He's enlisted to speak Senator Pagliari. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u75	Mr President, I believe that at this moment reflection must start from fundamental concepts. Democracy has been highly evoked in this House, but from an etymological point of view what is democracy but the government of the people, if not the instrument through which a community is governed by its members? This model, that is democracy, is realized in many ways (there is assembly democracy, there is participatory democracy) and it is obvious that representative democracy is also an instrument through which we try to give a rule so that a community can be governed and respect it can have directions, decisions and solutions of problems. In this context, it is clear that representative democracy is either strictly proportional or a model that seeks solutions for a country to be governable, and this is the real challenge facing those who want and care to protect democracy today. In this House, during these hours, we have heard statements, which must be respected as all are within the meaning of Article 21 of the Constitution, but which are little constructed with respect to the need to govern a country and to give it the Government, the alternation of opinions, projects and solutions. In fact, when in this debate the solution that emerges from the speeches is that we must not find a method by which there are those who govern and can govern, it is clear that we are not concerned about the fate of the country or the need to govern it, but simply to maintain a structure in which, since no one can govern, everyone can do so, which represents the failure of the last twenty years and the essential requirement of our democracy at this time. It is absolutely clear to us in the Democratic Party that, with all the delicacies that emerge from the solutions that are proposed, we must give Italy a system in which the government of the institutions is possible and that there are those who can govern. In this House we have come to the paradox that those who voted for this reform at first reading today say that constitutional reform must not be done. In this debate, at first reading and today at second reading, there are no alternative solutions, there are no alternative proposals; we are always at the mockery of the proposal that has been drafted and voted by the House, but a different solution has never emerged; the solution has always emerged to prevent something from changing, to maintain the status quo. I believe that for some political forces there is a clear contradiction with the programmes announced during the electoral campaigns, but also an inability to put themselves at stake with respect to a political framework that demands the ability to face a reform of the institutions that until a few months ago no one denied had to be done. No one, in fact, denied that bicameralism had to be overcome; no one denied that there was a problem of modifying the current bicameralism because it did not work, because there was too much slowness, because there was no adequate level of representativeness of the system. Today that a solution has been sought (through which there is only one Political Chamber, one Chamber that has the legislative function, and there is another that represents the territorial institutions), we are faced with the denial of the possibility of discussing a model, the denial of the evaluation of the model in its specific. I therefore believe that it should be made clear that, with regard to the forces that voted on this reform and that will vote on it tomorrow at the time of the final vote, there is, however, the assumption of responsibility to give this country a model of representation at parliamentary level that best represents the evolution that our system has had. In fact, I believe that the representative Chamber of territorial institutions, that is, the Senate, is an idea that is suited to the evolution that has had our institutional model in the substantial dynamics of relations between Regions, Provinces and Municipalities; this in highlighting the need for a greater protagonism of territorial institutions, but at the same time in detecting the need for a balance between these different levels of representation and protagonism; the need to have places where this confrontation does not simply pass through ideological dynamics or through appeals for conflict of attribution before the Constitutional Court, but also through places where there is a serious confrontation that can lead to the elaboration and definition of addresses and this can happen within the Senate as well as redefined. To rethink also the role of the Senate in the terms in which it emerges from the reform, thanks to the protagonism that the Senate certainly had on this level, is an attempt to modernise an institution, to re-evaluate it and to have a balance at the parliamentary level between different functions that are equally important because for the maintenance of an institutional framework, the efficiency of a system serves not only the decision-making function but also that of controlling active policies, the function of controlling the public dynamics enshrined in the reform of the Constitution. On this level I believe that the challenge before us is between those who want to try to change the system that is necessary and those who simply shout "to the wolf, to the wolf" but do not propose any different solution. (at 19:53) (Following Pagliari). It is also clear that all solutions are perfectible, but I have not seen any contributions aimed at perfecting the system. Let us look at the amendments that have been tabled, let us look at the substance of these amendments if they have proposed an alternative model and if they represent real amending proposals that improved the whole model. I believe, with all due respect to the opposition and all political forces, that this has not emerged in this branch of Parliament and that all this makes the difference between those who have assumed the responsibility to propose to the country a change of the model that did not work anymore and those who did not want to take it; between those who think that the need to save democracy is to give a ruling democracy, a democracy in which there are those who are called to carry out the function of governing and those who are called to provide an account to the electorate every five years and not to maintain a model that has shown all its limitations because the consociativism of which we have been victims for twenty years is not what this country needs. This country needs a clear, clear, coherent political dialectic that is subjected to electoral scrutiny and that assigns the role of the majority and the role of the opposition, in a dynamic that precisely because of the difficulty... PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. I invite you to conclude. Mr Pagliari (PD). ... of the context that must be governed, it is a dynamic that must have clear roles, clear positions, clear responsibilities. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u76	Senator Comaroli is registered to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u77	Madam President, we are moving towards the vote on the reform of the Constitution, but it must be pointed out that it is unacceptable for us, for me, to vote in favour for two reasons: firstly for the method used and secondly for the content. As far as the method is concerned, it is clear to everyone that something is wrong. The reconstruction of this method has already been done by many in this House, and especially by President Calderoli. In the course of the previous examinations, a number of regulations and practices were forced into force, and we have all had the opportunity to see it. Someone then explains to me the urgency of approving this constitutional bill tomorrow, considering that we have three months to do so. I cannot understand the real reasons, that is to say the obvious ones because perhaps then the reason why the reform is to be approved tomorrow (ready, now, now, is urgent) is the need to hold together a somewhat occasional majority and held together by the fact that Thursday we are going to vote on the Presidencies and vice-presidents of the Commissions and whatever else. Throughout this process it is important to show that we are moving, to show that we are dynamic and to say: do you see what we do? Unfortunately, the substance of what is being done does not matter anymore. The important thing is to make people look like they're doing something. We have lost sight of the foundation, what should be the pivotal law of our system, but it does nothing, it does not matter, the important thing is to make smoke. The expression that I used before, to show that something is moving, I used it specifically because unfortunately this we do not consider a serious reform because if you go to see in the contents we find that its start was determined by affirmations become commonplaces like, indeed, the ungovernability and slowness induced by the bicameral system. How many times have we heard Renzi go on television and say: see what I am doing, I give certainty to the government and give speed to the process of approving the laws. However, speaking of slow adoption of laws due to the bicameral system, this is a statement even without content. We saw in December what happened with the Jubilee decree. How long did it take us to approve it? So if there is will you do it. The problem is when some laws may not be made well and then it is in that case that time is needed. Let's also think about the question of the law of stability: we saw last year - I mean by the law of stability 2015 passed in December 2014 - that despite three passages, despite everything, there were errors in the law. Or in this year's law of stability, it was not possible at first reading to proceed to analyze some important issues because the Government was not ready, because the Government needed more time. So the statement "needs to speed up and make laws" is a statement that leaves the time it finds. If we then look at the statistical point of view, we also see that unfortunately, in our history, many laws have been made, many legends, too many. We actually have too many laws. So it is not necessary to speed up the legislative process because we do not have to rush the laws. If anything, it is necessary to make the laws better. Do less but do well. We remember, even recently, cases of laws made and then modified after one or two months, or laws filled with paragraphs and references to other laws, which for many citizens are sometimes incomprehensible. This whole system denotes that bureaucracy is hiding behind the laws, right behind the meanders of all these codes, to avoid taking responsibility. I repeat that the objective is not to make the laws quickly, but must be to do the laws well, to make laws that serve the citizens, unless - indeed - we want to do operations exclusively advertising, as is this reform. The cost cut that could be pursued - in our opinion - also by other methods, the intention of a more efficient legislative system denied then by the complex and cumbersome legislative procedure still in place, the relationship State-Regions that is not simplified at all or rationalized: here, these are the arguments that the Government has used in the last period to hide the real risk of the new centralism. In our view, in fact, the real objective of the reform is the shift of the institutional axis towards the Executive, towards Palazzo Chigi, towards Matteo Renzi. Above all it is in connection with the new electoral law because, if we go to see, ballot, majority prize to the single list, thresholds of access, blocked vote on the capilists will ensure that the Chamber of Deputies will be managed by the leader of the winning party, even if it will have few votes; in the electoral competition you will be able to use the system "a" takes everything, according to the model of the man only in command. In addition to the formation of laws, the election of the President of the Republic, the members of the Constitutional Court and the Supreme Council of the judiciary will also be affected. In fact, it is the opinion of many constitutionalists, and therefore not Silvana Comaroli, that Senate reform and electoral law create the conditions for an authoritarian turning point. Mauro Dogliani, professor of constitutional law at the University of Turin, for example, points out that this electoral method introduces an element of randomness or ungovernability, making it problematic to bring back to a popular will the true determinations of political direction. An election with majority prizes such as this will submit to an electorate more or less unaware of political offers elaborated elsewhere. We are at the limit of staying in the parliamentary system, because the body to which the function of political direction is assigned is no longer the Parliament or the Chamber of Deputies, that is, the whole of the parties representing the citizens called to determine national politics, but it will only be the Premier! As for the composition, the 100 senators, is flagged as the achievement of a stratospheric cost reduction because the vitalities and salaries of the senators will no longer be paid; but if we go to verify, there will be really little money saved. The reality is that the President-in-Office of the Council, Matteo Renzi, has created a clash of images on a populist terrain that was striking: it is obvious, in fact, that the citizens are affected if they say: We have cut costs, we save you, there will be no more waste, we will no longer pay the senators. So it is natural to ask why Members have not been reduced too - as, among other things, we have also proposed with our amendments - if that was the objective. No, ladies and gentlemen. He used the populist system to get to his real purpose: to command him over everything and all and trouble to those who disagree with him. A nod to mayor senators, too. I have seen several mayors do their duty, duty that requires commitment and time. When you want to do multiple things, in the end they all get hurt. This double role requires the mayors to be able to perform their old functions seriously and those of senator. I imagine then a mayor who will have to be the mayor of Milan, the president of the Metropolitan City and also the senator. Who knows how he's gonna do everything? It'll be Superman! In addition, by proceeding in the examination of the reform, we see that we also touch Title V. First, the constitutional provisions relating to the Provinces as the constituent body of the Republic are deleted. But be careful: the Provinces are not abolished. In fact, they survive, they have simply been changed the name: instead of "Province" they are called "Large Area." What has really been abolished is the provincial council, i.e. the elective body. What has been abolished is democracy and popular sovereignty! The legislative and regulatory competence between the State and the Regions has also been reviewed. In particular, the shared competence has been abolished, with a redistribution of matters between the State and the Region. Here again, for many constitutionalists it is a reform with punitive effects for the autonomy because, by virtue of a vague general assumption such as the national interest, the state and centralist supremacy emerges. The supremacy clause should be limited to exceptional, particular, temporally limited situations, with the exclusion of matters which should be assigned exclusively to the Regions. Otherwise, one day someone in the government might think that they would be given other powers by way of derogation from the Constitution, so much is the national interest and who can forbid them to do so? It has been said that doing so will eliminate many disputes between the State and the Regions, but unfortunately, if the objective was to eliminate the competing subjects, in fact the litigation will multiply because it is not expected for the Regions what the exclusive subjects are and in any case there will always be a mix between state competence and regional competence. This reform, Mr President, in our view, is only necessary to deceive the citizens. Or it is necessary for Premier Matteo Renzi, to have a commercial, to show, as his motto is now, as he is good, as he is beautiful and how many things he does. Although then we see that what he says is all smoke. Trying to make a serious reform was possible and not even difficult, given that the idea of amending the Constitution has been developed for 20 years and that even the last reform included, for example, the reduction of costs and the reduction of senators and Members. But there is a desire to do so, precisely because it is the reform of the Constitution, it must be carefully evaluated and shared, not decided by the Government. Just a serious change would have been able to really reduce the costs of politics and to give more attention to the content of the laws, than to the spot of the moment. We have also seen it recently, in the case of civil servants and card fraud: we immediately saw the bill ad that we intend to approve, in order to make sure that they can be fired in 48 hours, because we need to "show" the citizens. Another serious reform would have been to restore national sovereignty, marginalizing foreign influence in internal decisions and perhaps Renzi, in these days, knows something about the European Union's interference in the facts concerning our policy. Just in case! A serious reform would have been able to apply more federalism, to promote the different instances of the territory and would have been able to really strengthen the tools to guarantee popular sovereignty, instead of saying that the future Senate will be the Senate of the self-governments, when, if we go to see its actual competences, it will be little or nothing and its function will be reduced to a marginal function. It is said, however, that we will have the Senate of the autonomous, in which the Regions and the Municipalities will be able to assert their demands. I challenge anyone to say what will be the instances that will actually bring to the attention of the Government. In all this, the drama is that, in the end, the participation of citizens does not find space in this constitutional reform that, using the words of the constitutionalist Villone to some extent overturns those principles that were aimed at participation, unless it is thought that it is worth, for the purpose of participation, the participation of tweets and Facebook pages. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u80	Senator Martelli, who I consider to be a senator of the Republic, is registered to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u81	Madam President, I take this as a compliment, but I consider myself a citizen who temporarily occupies these posts. So, for starters, I will not go so far in trying to disassemble the content of this reform for the third time. I will just say that all the objections raised in three rounds of debate have never been answered. When you don't give an answer, you can assume that the answer isn't there; it's better to keep quiet, if you can't say anything good, right? However, I would like to recall the concepts for a moment. Number one: it is not true that this constitutional reform will speed up the legislative process, because, when the new Senate makes use of the power to examine a bill, it will run seventy-five days before it can end. Now, with a decree-law, it takes sixty; so we are more than 25 percent in terms of the use of time and therefore it does not speed up. Secondly, if it were necessary to speed up, what is the point? To make more laws? But we already have so many. And what is the value of having so many laws? Each law corrects the mistakes of the previous ones. The law of stability had not yet fulfilled ten days and was already touched. Not even ten days. The most important law of a state, the only law that the government is entitled to do (because the law of stability is a government law by definition), already after ten days had problems. Unbelievable! She was born badly. She had a congenital malformation. So it is not true that we must make the laws quickly. Democracies as dignified as ours do not have all this legislative production; there is no need to have a hasty democracy, because there is no need to make so many laws and, in any case, the instrument of the decree-law allows you to make them. However, we produce more laws than others produce. And it is not true - Senator Pagliari is no longer in the Chamber, so I cannot address him directly - that we need a granite majority and a guaranteed government to be able to make laws quickly. Even when there was the pentapartite and other things of this type and the Governments lasted what lasted, there were many laws made the same, demonstrating that it is not true the sillogism for which, with a strong and cohesive majority and with a single Chamber that does things, you go faster. That's not true, because there are counter-examples. That's all I need: a counter-example is enough for me. But something more must be said, namely that this reform is illogical. Why is it illogical? Because he proposes things that are inconsistent with other things he proposes. For example, he proposes to make a Senate that should represent territorial entities. That would make sense if the latter had powers that they don't have. We are dealing with regions that are entities with derivative finance: if Mother State does not give him money, they do nothing. Then, when I claim to give voice to regional self-government, I have to give it some more power. Instead, I do the opposite: the supremacy clause, the superpowers of the government. He already has superpowers with the decree-law and is now given superpowers; in any case, he has the last word. That's illogical. Now I'm gonna make a few references to illogicality, then we're gonna see what the picture is because, when you find out that what's being put under your nose is illogical and is being carried forward anyway, it means that there's a bigger design and you have to look at it in another way. This is the first consideration of illogicality. The second is that there is already no equal bicameralism. There would be if and only the two Houses had the same number and were elected in the same way and had the same active and passive electorate. Instead, not everyone who is eligible for the Senate is also in the House: you must be over forty years old. To vote in the Senate, you must have twenty-five and not eighteen. Thus, in itself, the representative composition is different in number and the Senate is elected on a regional basis, with a regional majority prize. It already contains a germ of regional representativeness. But in that case, either you make the move or you don't. Make the move to say: "I want to represent the autonomy and then stop it becomes an inconsistent and counterproductive thing. Examples of what a federal senate means are there. I think, for example, of the United States of America, which are born federal immediately. There are clear competences of the Senate and another thing is also clear. Since you have to represent the counter-power of a strong power, you need a strong Senate. In fact, the Senate is stronger than Congress: the senators last longer; they have more powers and more prestige. They have a whole series of collateral. Also, in a system where you accentuate power, you need to put the Constitution in a safe. For this I thank you for teaching us, and Uncle Silvio before you, what are the easy access keys to the Constitution: it does not take a large majority to touch it and, moreover, the referendum does not even have a quorum. It would suffice for 51% of 40% to vote. In some referendums that was the number. 50 percent of 40 percent is 20 percent. With 20% we would change the Constitutional Charter. In the United States of America - for someone I am repeating myself because I have already said so in the Commission today - it takes 75 of Congress and Senate and 75 percent of States must say yes, regardless of their populace. The small state votes and weighs as much as the big state and is right. Instead, you, in your division of seats, have not even thought about this. You have not even taken a virtuous example, which is that of a consolidated federal state. By the way, it is the federal state that should have a constitution that should be marched, according to what Mr. Matteo Renzi says, since it is well over two hundred years old. Ours, which does not even have seventy, according to him is already to be changed, forgetting that it has been changed only about twenty times in the previous years. The important thing is to say only one piece of truth; so, whoever listens fills the void of truth with what you expect to put in it. What is this model that you want to propose? It's the Polish one, according to which the Senate is there, but it doesn't matter. But it's a waste of time, in your mindset. It advocates the laws; it thinks a little about them and proposes amendments. Then the House says: I don't care about merchandise; nothing is done. This is the model. It's not original; it's badly copied. Let's move on. Let's level up with the concept of the Constitution. The Constitution serves to give more power to citizens. The Constitution is born when you leave absolutism, when you move from a monarchy to a constitutional monarchy and you make a Constitution. The new form of government is called constitutional monarchy. This is taught in history and not in constitutional law. The Constitutions have this purpose: to take away power from governments and give it to citizens. It's a quote from Benjamin Franklin. Let us consider some other Constitutions because you, in fact, have a model. It is - if you like - one of the three versions of the Constitution of the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics. Those who call you communists in a certain sense are right. Analyzing them shows the difference between a Western and a Soviet Constitution. The Soviet Constitution does not cover all those parts of individual rights because in that type of state the interest of the many prevailed over that of the few. Right or acceptable, that's it. The Constitution was concerned with regulating relations between the various organizations of the state, rather than the individual citizen. Second thing. Those Constitutions, by their very nature, tended to take a photograph of what was there, i.e. they took note of what had become the State and of the relations between the organizations of the State. You are doing this; you are saying that, since the process of the laws, according to you, does not proceed fast enough, this is the antidote. Since, in your opinion, there is too much inertia with two Chambers, then the antidote is practically to remove one. You are, that is, doing the same thing: photograph a situation and say that a Constitution is needed to guarantee this state of affairs. That's what you're saying. So, in fact, you have a Bolshevik mold within your constitution. It is a pity that all that part of solidarity and mutualism in a state of that kind, which you do not even contemplate, is missing. We are still up to the level, because, given all these inconsistencies, the question that would be asked is why we want to do the same. Then, as in the book "Flatlandia" in which one tries to describe a three-dimensional world for someone who is in two dimensions - who cannot conceive it - one has to climb up and enter a higher dimension and look at it from above. In this they helped me all your calls to what is the new world order. After you hear it once, two, three times, you wonder why. Who spoke for the first time in the world? It was in 1944 at the Bretton Woods Conference; it was the first time that this was discussed. As a world order it was meant an organization of trade in monetary flows that would result in a political consequence. That's how he was born. After that, how did you get on with it? With the implementation of this world order. We see it because it's exactly consistent with what you're doing. The first implementation was with GATT, the first free trade agreement, which was then replaced by the WTO, the World Trade Organisation. The International Monetary Fund was created at that time. This network had the following purpose: to create bodies not only over national but over-Union, which are above - at the highest level - of all and contain the famous international court, the one that settles disputes between states and unions of states and subjects who, in whatever way, have something to say (such as: you don't want me to sell junk food because it hurts, I bring you into question). International arbitration is above national legislation, above that of the European Union, above that of the United States; in short, it stands at the top: this is the hierarchy of command. From there, through free trade treaties (TTIP, TPP, NAFTA), you get to a second level. These regulate relations no longer at world level but of macro-areas: the European Union with the United States, the Asian bloc with the North American bloc. There too there are courts, international bodies - which do not comply with the laws of the States - which decide: they make decisions and the state has to give up. This, in turn, in the hierarchy, is based on the laws of the European Union, the United States and then of the individual States; so the state, which is the lowest of all, becomes a paper-card. I do not say this, but some economists, including Rogoff, who is a very famous international economist, and another one called Rodrik, who basically said the following: following this hierarchical chain of command, we realize that, in a context of globalization of trade and currency exchanges, there are only two possibilities: we make a planetary federation, therefore a planetary federal state, or a softer way, that is, the State renounces a certain type of legislation, that is, its function must become to create the context so that there is no obstacle to trade and currency flows. Let's take a good look at her. This Constitution does exactly that: it cuts democracy away, it reduces everything to a government that still exists, that doesn't have too many intermediaries, and that takes orders from above. We are already seeing it: we have a government that takes orders from the European Union, which in turn takes them from these international bodies that are ultimately the ones who dictate the rules. Here are the statements of JpMorgan, of Moody's: they are those organisms that have been created as collateral (business banks are born as collateral), as guardians of this system. You see, it all works out. We can also understand why we are doing favors to the banks, to the financial system, because the financial system is the one that supports the finance of governments, of those who in turn support the International Monetary Fund, which is one of the puppeteers of all this castle. Banks are essential bodies when they continue to ask for favors because the Boschi bank has been asked for favors (trusts granted freely); the Intesa bank and Unicredit have been asked for favors, or Montepaschi. For example: "Buy me, please, the Tirreno Power by De Benedetti, by the way; buy me Tirenno Power that stands for bankruptcy." And so the Monte dei Paschi, which has 20 billion unquenchable credits, made three increases in capital and has the shares that are worth less than the paper on which they are printed, bought the Tirreno Power, a company decorated and in bankruptcy. Is it clear that by asking this something you have to give and what you give? For example, 7.5 billion with the decree-law IMU-Bankitalia that you will remember. It's a do ut des. That's the pattern. The pattern based on a hierarchical chain. Once you understand this and take a good look from above it is obvious that this constitutional reform must be rejected, but not because there is a democratic issue because that aspect, looking at the whole, becomes secondary. The real question is the compression of higher freedoms. You'll have someone you don't even know which side he's on. So it is no longer a question of democracy. We'll have someone who doesn't even know which side it's decided everything for you; they didn't just decide the form of government of your country, or how to choose the constitutional judges or the President of the Republic. He must have decided exactly your route: from the cradle to the grave. How you eat, how you work, what your pension is. That is what is underneath, and that is why this reform must be rejected. Anyway, I could get to say that I was joking, that maybe I wasn't. But it would be interesting if he tried to disassemble what I said. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u83	Madam President, Mr President-in-Office of the Government, ladies and gentlemen, a person who has read the current Constitution, who appreciates it and knows its genesis, in the face of the constitutional text before us, can only remain with a smile; a bitter smile because of the superficiality with which many of the issues it contains have been addressed. What we are about to vote on remains a messy reform, sometimes unfinished both in terms of roles and powers. Only the title of the bill remains very appealing. It is a clever title because it argues that the text contains provisions for overcoming bicameralism, the reduction of the number of parliamentarians, the reduction of costs, the functioning of the institutions, the abolition of the CNEL, the revision of Title V of Part II of the Constitution. A title that contains a real fraud and is dangerous because it constitutes - and that is why I have mentioned it - also the question on which citizens will be called to speak with a yes or a no. In fact, none of the topics listed are dealt with and resolved definitively. The bicameralism of equality is not surpassed, but a Senate is created which is a sort of limp duck. A Senate that should represent the territorial institutions and therefore a strengthening of the autonomy, through the appointment or election of representatives of Regions and Municipalities, just when the competences to the autonomous are reduced and mortified. Senators are elected indirectly and therefore citizens will not be able to choose them directly, but will be elected by the Regional Councils; they will be chosen or, in the best way, indicated by the parties. Just as citizens will not be able to choose those of the House, which are included in special lists chosen by the parties, by virtue of an electoral law, the Italicum, approved by the PD with the vote of confidence. If it is true that the number of Members is reduced, the most disturbing aspect is that the cost is not reduced. Because - it goes without saying - work in Rome will still have to be paid in some way, as well as transfers. As well as the management costs of the structure remain. It reduces the number of Members, but to a very lesser extent than we proposed with our constitutional reform, reducing the total number of Members of the House as well. The 95 senators elected by the Regional Councils, and not directly by the citizens, will be called to duplice positions and the most skilled will also have triple positions (Common, Region and Senate). So much so that, in order to be sure that they will come to Rome, an amendment had to be made to Article 64 of the Constitution, which lays down the obligation for Members of Parliament to participate in the meetings of the Assembly and in the work of the Commissions. To the Senators of regional nomination will be added the five Senators of presidential nomination that last in office seven years, plus the former Presidents of the Republic, who become it by right. While the constituents had foreseen the presence of the senators for life as wise in a Senate of elders, now the ratio of the presence of the wise senators called to advise those who will be simple regional advisors free to go out to the Senate for two days a week. It really sounds like a joke if it wasn't written in a constitutional bill. How two or three assignments can be met simultaneously is then absolutely difficult to comprehend. And then a question above all: what is the use of a Senate composed largely of regional councillors, as well as mayors, if the competences of the autonomouss are completely emptied? Today that the matter of exclusive regional competence or competitor with the State is very wide, to confront with the choices of the Government is sufficient the unified Conference. A unified conference that costs a few thousand euros a year will replace a Senate that, to do many, much less things, costs a few hundred million euros. It might as well have been worth completely abolishing it, as we of Forza Italia have repeatedly argued. Also because this was the promise made by Renzi in the electoral campaign for the primaries of PD. Tomorrow that the competences of the Regions provided for in Article 117 of the Constitution will be practically non-existent, to confront with the Government on those matters will be the Senate of the Republic through the so-called bicameral laws. This is absurd in legal and practical terms. Regions without constitutional competence. Municipalities without resources, as a result of cuts in local finance. Provinces that change name, becoming bodies of wide area, but keeping intact the overlap with many of the competences of the other two territorial authorities. We are faced with an authentically banal text, as well as incomplete. We are faced with a lack of coordination between the constitutional rules. We are faced with rules that will complicate the life of Italians, rather than simplify it. The Democratic Party is carrying out a series of reforms by majority vote, although, as I said, with a majority prize declared illegitimate by the Constitutional Court. With the electoral law he even avoided the debate and posed the question of trust, the first case in republican history. Thanks to this constitutional change and the new electoral law a party that is a minority in the country can govern, make appointments, elect guarantee bodies, elect the President of the Republic. The procedure for designating the new Senate is certainly confused. The role of representatives of the Regions in the Senate is ambiguous. The functions assigned to the new Senate are inconsistent. The division of competences between the State and the Regions leads to a neocentralism and certainly to a new constitutional dispute promoted by the Regions. The costs of politics do not really decrease. The reform does not address the challenges for the modernisation of the country. A reform must represent a common heritage of all the Italian people, not a moment of division. The President of the Council, never legitimized by elections, intended to politicize and personalize the confirmation referendum. In April 1947, the first government around Christian Democracy was formed, with the Communist Party and the Socialist Party in opposition. That should be the case in democracy; if we are not in democracy it is another thing. Moreover, the normal thing would be that senator mayors were elected by citizens, or - at most - by other mayors. On the other hand, the reform provides for them to be elected by the regional councillors. Think about this: we are talking about regional councillors, who in the first phase of the law's application will not have been elected to serve as senator. The citizens - yes - voted for them, however, thinking that they should be the regional councillors and not the senators, the senator-designators or the senator-mayor-designers. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u99	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, representatives of the Government, I believe that there is a definition that, more than many others, can indicate what this reform is. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u100	Senator Ceroni is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u101	Mr President, Mr Undersecretaries, ladies and gentlemen, the Constitution is the fundamental Charter of the values and principles that characterize a State. The Italian Constitution, which entered into force on 1 January 1948, was voted by a very large majority. It was the result of a long confrontation between all the political forces present at the moment in Parliament and the result of so many battles, so many social struggles, so many humiliations, suffering and sacrifices. The claim to change it in the ways and forms we are witnessing shows a despotic, overbearing and arrogant attitude and raises many concerns both in the method and in the substance. In the method, first of all, constitutional reforms cannot be the subject of coup d'état, but they must be born in a peaceful political climate and in a truly open and constructive confrontation, aimed at improving the text and ensuring that choices are widely shared. I think it is entirely inappropriate to proceed with constitutional reform by majority vote because we cannot ignore the fact that the parliamentary composition of the current legislature is the result of the fact that a very large number of Members and Senators sit in Parliament as a result of a majority award declared unconstitutional by the judgment of Court No 1 of 2014. We cannot forget that this majority is held up by a mass of Members who have been elected by the citizens in another coalition with values and objectives completely different from those you carry out. In addition, a constitutional reform must be the result of a parliamentary initiative in accordance with the procedure laid down in Article 138 of the Constitution, while this reform, in essence, has been written and imposed by the Government to a majority that is now servant and slave and accustomed to coups of confidence to vote for everything the Government wants, under the threat of going to the early elections. So much for the principle of separation of legislative and executive powers! All the more so because this is an illegitimate Government, led by a President who has not even been a candidate for elections; a Government born in contempt of popular sovereignty. In democracy it is the citizens who choose their rulers, not the conspiracy of palace. On the merits, I am absolutely opposed to a single-camera system. The second reading of the legislative texts allows for more detailed work; the thought of experts, the opinion of the public, the technicians of the Senate, the work in the Commission and then those in the Chamber, avoid a mountain of legislative castroneries. It is clear that in democracy Parliament is no longer made up of lawyers and a second reading is absolutely necessary and appropriate. The need to shorten the legislative process is a pretext for achieving the same results by intervening on Parliament's regulations. If you really wanted to cut costs, you might as well wipe out the Senate completely. However, while we wanted to eliminate bicameralism as equal, we needed to provide for an adequate system of weights and counterweights. For example, it would have been a good solution to foresee the direct election of the President of the Republic and to give him other powers. The new composition of the Senate, transformed into a non-elective body composed of 74 regional councillors, 21 mayors and 5 senators for life, also raises doubts. I do not know how the mayors of the capital cities and regional councillors will carry out both functions. An idea of this kind could only come to those who never made the regional councilor or the mayor. I overlook the decision to entrust to the senators (not elected) the constituent function, the possibility of electing the President of the Republic, the possibility of electing the constitutional judges, which seem to me constitutional idiocies. A monocameral system, with an electoral law awarding a majority prize to a single party that alone can elect the President of the Republic and 10 of the 15 constitutional judges, risks bringing Italy under the dictatorship of a minority representing less than one seventh of those entitled to vote. Finally, I believe that with your reform, instead of addressing the issue of the functionality of the state, to modify together rules that allow a modern and fluid functioning of its joints, you are choosing to dismantle the very structure of the country. The Regions, with the changes made to Title V of the Constitution, will lose part of their competences; moreover, thanks to the haste and superficiality with which the revision of the competences between State and Regions has been made, the residual ones are often confused and contradictory, determining copious conflicts of competences (more than in the past). The Provinces, first with the so-called Delrio law and now with their deletion from the Constitution, have become second-level administrative bodies, unelected, entirely in the hands of the local political power, without any form of popular control. The municipalities, increasingly impoverished by the cuts wanted by the last governments, are paralyzed and are losing the ability to really intervene in the lives of their citizens, even in the face of emergencies. With this reform, with the electoral law you passed, with the new RAI law, which allows the government to appoint the top public service leaders, we easily realize that the funeral of democracy is being organised. You are building an unbalanced system for the Commission, without counterweights, with a useless Senate that cannot participate in trust. With a government in fact master of the constitutional system that can choose the President of the Republic and choose directly 10 of the 15 constitutional judges. I believe that our party, despite all the goodwill and all the willingness, can only take note of your attitude of arrogance, arrogance and protection in wanting to carry out this law of unholy constitutional change at all costs and without consent. You want the fight at all costs: confrontation on the Constitution, confrontation on de facto unions. You are caught up in a delirium of omnipotence, while the economic situation is that of a country in serious difficulties, in default: companies that close, stagnation, rampant unemployment, banking system to collapse. The situation in our country would require moderation, prudence, caution and responsibility. Instead, you go ahead, take the country to a bitter measure, to break into a referendum. A referendum that you are forced to do because Article 138 of the current Constitution requires it, if the constitutional change is not approved by two thirds of the members of each of the Houses. I'm guessing what you're gonna do. You will play with expressions that go fishing in the populist imagination, such as reducing the costs of the system by deceiving citizens once again. The real economy is done if the Chambers work, if they carry out their duty, if they solve the problems of the country. But don't get your hopes up. You will have to face the referendum on your own, against everyone because you have managed to compact oppositions, even those that are on diametrically opposed positions. You'll lose the referendum. The referendum will be your grave. What I'm sorry is that in the meantime you will also bring Italy to the grave. [[Applause from the FI-PdL Group XVII]]
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u102	Senator Cotti is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u103	Mr President, it has already been said several times, but it is still worth remembering: this constitutional reform does not at all save on the costs of the institutions. The cut represented by the lower number of salaries to be paid to senators amounts to a figure close to 10 percent of the total costs of the Senate. There would remain the costs for the structures, for the staff, for institutional activities, for pensions, even those to pay for the trips of former senators. The real meaning of this reform is the centralization of power. Only one House that counts, elected with abundant majority prize and with control over the election of the Head of State and Constitutional Court. Meanwhile, the number of signatures needed for the repealing referendums increases, a clear signal of insufferance for participatory democracy. The Senate will only maintain some real functions. But would it not have been better to abolish it directly, or cut costs by cutting wages and numbers of both senators and MPs, according to the proposal of the 5 Star Movement Group? The text describing the new role of the Senate reminds me of an old model of Parliament that brings us back six hundred and sixty years. It was February 1355 when Alfonso IV of Aragon reunited the first Sardinian Parliament. Built on the Aragonese Catalan model, the Sardinian Parliament, more widely called "the Courts," was constituted by three arms called stations: there was the ecclesiastical station, formed by archbishops, bishops, abbots and priors; the military station, formed by nobles, knights and feudal men and the royal station, composed of representatives elected by the cities not infeudated and therefore directly dependent on the royal power. The stations had consultative power, as their deliberations became effective only if accepted by the reigning sovereign. If we examine the role of the new Senate of the Italian Republic, we read, in the new Article 70 of the Constitution, that: Any bill approved by the Chamber of Deputies shall be transmitted immediately to the Senate of the Republic which, within ten days, at the request of a third of its members, may arrange to examine it. In the following thirty days, the Senate of the Republic may decide on proposals for amendment of the text, on which the Chamber of Deputies shall give its final ruling. Practically the Senate can ask to discuss anything, but, with the exception of some limited issues, it will be decided by the House. Well, even in 1355 the Sardinian Parliament could discuss everything it wanted, even deliberate, and without having to make it require one third of the members. But then he decided the ruler. From 1355 to 2016 the ruler would be replaced by a chamber of MPs chosen by party leaders (the capilists, who will constitute the majority of the elected, do not need the vote of preference). The elected, under penalty of marginalization and non-rewarding, will obey their head of party/head of government, which will in fact take on a political importance comparable to that of Alfonso IV of Aragon. And who's gonna elect the senators? The regional councils, with a mechanism that protects the largest party. In fact, the party system captures the choice of representatives of the people. If you want to give the Senate a role of control of the House of Deputies, here is that the choice of controllers is skillfully entrusted to the party system that expresses those who should be controlled. Here again the new Parliament recalls the Sardinian one of 1355: then the most influential members of the arm, the military one, were appointed by the sovereign. If this reform is approved and the confirmation referendum will pass, we will come closer to the times of Alfonso IV of Aragon. I already seem to see, imagining this House in the future, in the face of our President-in-Office, the appearance of the Marquis of Villasòr. President, are you the Marquis of Villasor? Or maybe it's the Marquis of Laconi? These were, in fact, the usual presidents of the Sardinian military station. Perhaps one day our parliamentarians will also be forced, as the last resort of a Parliament that is struggling to free the people from the powerful in turn, to formulate the fateful five questions to the sovereign, as happened twice in the history of that old Parliament. But can five questions be asked to the sovereign in the new Renza-style Parliament? Marquis of Villasòr... I'm sorry, ma'am, I'm done. [[Applause from the M5S Group]]
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u104	It's just that if you want to be president, you have to be regional councilor or mayor.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u105	Senator Campanella is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u106	Mr President, we have listened to and will listen to in the next few hours to a catalogue of arguments against this revision of the Constitution, so much so that, in imagining this speech, I have tried to understand how I could ask my colleagues to think about it, if anyone among them wanted to reconsider their position, then their favour of this so-called reform, taking into account the alteration that it produces in the balance between the powers that are at the basis of every liberal Constitution, which guarantees a balance that must return to all citizens. As a result of this reform, the Chambers will no longer play their fundamental role, which is to represent the citizens. For decades, attempts have been made to attack the 1948 Constitution in order to shift the balance in favour of the Commission. Last but not least, the centre-right has tried it, and on that occasion the colleagues of the Democratic Party were against it. They now find themselves approving a revision of the Constitution which, beyond the letter and beyond the mechanisms, reproduces that imbalance in favour of the Commission which empties Parliament of substance. In addition to the battle we have fought and will continue to do until the last minute in Parliament, we are obviously preparing for the opposing referendum on this law, calling the citizens to vote not on the person of the President of the Council who, let's say, is very little compared to the institutional architecture of our country, but on the changes in the mechanisms of government of the Italian Republic that this reform would produce where - God forbid - it should come into force. This struggle that we will bring to the streets is a struggle of ideas to let our fellow citizens know what is being done in Parliament. We already know that she will not have friends with all the main mainstream sources of information, televisions for example, that for the laws that have followed in recent years are substantially under the control of party organizations. So we should do a job on the street, talking to people and informing them of the actual content of this revision law. Tonight, however, I made the choice to be a spokesperson. I am convinced of the role of representative of the MP but I found myself last night, in Catania, listening to a boy of twenty-one years who, in presenting this reform to the defendants at the first meeting for the constitution of the committee for the no of the Sicilian Region, expressed, with a mastery and competence (with love, I would say) for the Constitution a series of considerations that I felt right to represent to this Parliament and therefore virtually to the whole of Italy. I'll read them to you: We live in times undoubtedly difficult, dark times for our democracy and for the values on which our civil coexistence was founded. Already with the sunset of the first Republic, with the transition from the electoral system proportional to that of the majority, there has been a surreptitious change in the form of government in our country. Changing the electoral law means potentially having a significant impact on the quality of the democratic life of the State, even without a formal amendment of the Constitutional Charter. From the nineties onwards, not by chance, our country has seen ever weaker parliaments and ever stronger governments (just think about the abuse of the decree of urgency) - which, as you will remember, colleagues, many and many times we have denounced - with artificially constructed majorities through stratagems contained in electoral laws of progressively majority nature: the Mattarellum, first, then the Porcellum and, last, even worse, the Italicum. Majoritarian laws and mechanisms that empty the representation of meaning in order to aspire to such acclaimed governance. Let me close here: governability is useful, but probably what this country needs most is government, which is something else. It is not an abstract capacity, but an effective, concrete action in place; choices that, made in the interests of the citizens and not in the interests of economic groups, improve their state of life and solve the critical issues that history proposes. Instead, here you have sought - I remember since the time of Craxi and perhaps even before (I was then a boy) - the governability. I'm going back to the boy's words. "Democracy has become a merely formal value, an ornament completely devoid of an authentic, participatory, substantial content, as if we were living in the Republic of Democratic Aesthetics, and all this alienates citizens from politics, increases the distance between representatives and representatives and nourishes anti-politics and various populisms, throwing disturbing shadows on the future of our country." Let me make one more point: various populisms and various anti-politics. I urge everyone to pay attention to how anti-political the story of our President of the Council, when he refers, as an argument for the transformation of the Senate of the Republic into the Senate of the autonomous, to the reduction of the costs of politics. The highest costs of politics are the lack of government and the malaise of the citizens. This should be clear to anyone who approaches to rule this country: do not make anti-politics an instrument of government, because every time this happened it was dark times. Back to the boy's words: The constitutional reform of the Renzi Government, the so-called Boschi bill, in conjunction with the new electoral law (better known as Italicum), changes in veiled form the form of government of our country and even its form of state, transforming it into a personalistic and authoritarian regime. An electoral law that gives to a minority the government of the country, with a majority prize that is attributed to the first round to those who will get at least 40% of the votes; if, as is likely, no one will reach that threshold, there will be a ballot between the two most voted lists, independent of the number of votes obtained in the first round. This, in order to know who will rule the very evening of the vote, as Renzi loves to repeat. It is a transition from a representative democracy to a democracy of investiture and plebiscyticity. In a parliamentary republic voters elect their representatives in parliament, not the government. Let's get this over with with with these frequent misunderstandings. In Italy, at least formally, the people do not directly elect the Executive but Parliament, which will vote or then deny the trust of governments. With Italicum, on the other hand, ours will in essence become a presidential Republic. Among other things, the new electoral law does not seem to adhere to the censures of the Constitutional Court in judgment No. 1 of 2014, which declared the so-called Porcellum unconstitutional with its abnormal majority prize, which distorts the element of representation, fundamental in every democracy. And this Parliament, elected by a constitutionally illegitimate electoral law, has not only been dissolved by the President of the Republic, not only by law on ordinary administration, but even has the boldness and boldness to change the Constitution, the social pact on which our life is based in society - let me add: that Constitution according to which the system of election of this Parliament has been declared illegitimate - the Senate will be made up of 95 members elected by the regional councils and will no longer vote for the government; it will no longer be a body elected by the citizens or with members indicated by them, but it will be partitioned among the various parties present in the regional assemblies. I would add that the citizens will give an indication and the regional councillors will have to stick to it. At this point one wonders: but either they are totally sticking to it, and therefore it is an absolutely useless passage, or they do not stick exactly to the indications of the citizens, and then it is a passage that fakes representation and annuls it, and we are talking about a Senate that has supreme legislative powers, waiting for it to be entitled to express itself on the laws of constitutional revision. The boy continues: In addition, the legislative process, despite the rhetoric of simplification promoted by the Government, will become decidedly more complicated: some laws will be approved by both Chambers; others by the Chamber of Deputies alone; in some cases the new Senate will be able to request a new resolution that the House can override, re-approving the law by absolute majority. In short, in order to overcome the equal bicameralism, a confused and messy system is created, which will probably generate a vast dispute between the Chambers in front of the Consulta, slowing down the legislative process rather than accelerating it. In fact, a minority of the country, thanks to Italicum, will become a majority government. The Commission, with the Renzi-Boschi reform, will then be able to do the good and the bad time, expelling Parliament from its functions, even being able to elect the guarantee bodies of our constitutional system, without any respect for minorities. I recall that during the first discussion in this House, it was asked that guarantees for minorities should be included in the text of the Constitution. The majority wanted - and in this sense voted - the rules for the protection of the majority to be accepted in the parliamentary regulations and, therefore, to be granted, obtained by the majority. I take back the reading of the boy's intervention: It is a sinister scenario of the personalization of politics, which goes far beyond the Berlusconism and the renzism we are accustomed to: the demagogic politics of the leader will become a systemic element of our order. A breakdown of the system, an absolute primevalate very dangerous for the country, that echoes, although in different forms, what happened under fascism. Let me note that the state described by the boy is very similar to what we are already living. Today, however, we are in a position (and do so) to try to force and resist the will of the majority, referring to the constitutional dictation. After the reform, this resistance will be, essentially, not only useless, but also no longer based on the Constitutional Charter. The boy continues: "Italicum does not seem so unlike the Acerbo law, Mussolini's majority electoral law in 1923, which allowed the National Fascist Party to obtain a solid parliamentary majority and establish a dictatorial regime in our country." It has to be said that this young man, who is 21 years old, did not study in the good school done by this government, but he studied before and so he found teachers, who were able to describe to him, with a certain punctuality, what happened, at a stage in our country that we tend to forget. On the contrary, I would like to point out something curious: some of my Sicilian fellow citizens contacted me and told me that, in Acireale, the mayor for April 25 decided to make a new edition of the carnival. We have reached this point, namely that April 25 is only a day off and has lost all civil significance, so much so that a politician - because a mayor is a citizen, but also a politician, a man engaged in politics - faced, among other things, with the protests of the ANPI - this did not happen without anyone noticing - decided to continue to transform April 25 into a carnival day. I continue reading the boy's intervention: In the 1950s there was a general mobilization against the scam law, which awarded a majority prize to those who had reached 50 percent plus one of the votes. In short, it gave a bonus of seats to an absolute majority and did not give an absolute majority of seats to a minority of the country. For the record, in the 1953 elections the prize did not start, and the law was repealed the following year due to the wide controversy it had aroused. I mean, they were amateurs compared to the current government. Today almost all scholars of constitutional law agree that there are two forms of political power: the constituent power and the established powers. The constituent power is that power - which tends to be unlimited - which gives rise to the Constitution, outlining the essential features and values on which the order is based. In the Italian legal system he was embodied by the Constitutional Assembly, elected on 2 and 3 June 1946. The powers established are those powers which derive their legitimacy from the Constitution. The referendum institutions are the most direct way of enabling the people to exercise their sovereign role. It is a serious damage to the democratic principle and to equality.
