ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u120	He's enlisted to speak Senator Piccoli. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u121	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen present, Mr President of the Government, among the many critical points that this reform of the Constitution presents, I would like to briefly comment on an aspect which is the lowest common denominator of the draft revision. I am referring to the relationship between the centre and periphery, between the State on the one hand and Regions and local authorities on the other; a relationship on which until now has deeply affected the reform of Title V of the Constitution, approved by the Prodi Government in 2001; a new structure designed to develop the territories and enhance the diversity that characterizes from North to South our peninsula and which soon produced the negative effects that those norms have generated: heterogeneity of the regulatory disciplines on the same subject, uneven rights and protections for citizens, deresponsibility in the use of resources, institutional conflicts between the State and the Regions. There was therefore an urgent need for a profound rethink of the system within which the central and peripheral institutions relate. I would like to remind you that, in accordance with Article 114 of the Constitution, the municipalities, provinces, metropolitan cities, regions and the state are defined as constituent bodies of our Republic. The order of this short list is not accidental, but it aims to give prominence to the institutions closest to the citizens, to those institutions that at first instance deal with the more concrete issues relating to the life of all Italians. From this point of view, the dignity conferred by Constitutional Law No 3 of 2001 on local authorities, rewriting Article 114, is perhaps the most positive legacy of that constitutional revision. Today, however, we are witnessing a reversal of the march in which the password "centralize" brings back the hands of many years: centralize eliminating the specificities of the territories, centralize and conform to the unique thought of Renzia. Election law, RAI reform, constitutional reform and public administration reform are tangible examples. The present reform plan addresses the problems that over the years have stratified with an approach diametrically opposed to what remains written in the Constitution. It would have been appropriate to provide for a rationalization of the mechanisms by which the system should be corrected, to disseminate virtuous local experiences and to disseminate good territorial practices throughout the national territory. On the other hand, a neo-centralist approach has been chosen by providing for the centralisation of powers towards the State and the penalisation of territorial authorities. And this conclusion can be reached by considering, on the one hand, the overcoming of bicameralism equal, with the Senate that should become the Chamber of representation of the territorial institutions and of connection between the latter and the State and the European Union; on the other, recalling the new reorganization of the legislative competences between the State and the Regions. From the point of view of functions, parliamentary passages have reduced the list of powers conferred on the new Senate. We agree to differentiate the Senate from the Political Chamber which votes for the government's trust, but differentiate presupposes the attribution of new, different but effective powers. The spirit with which this reform should be conceived, and on which, at least in words, there is politically transversal convergence, is that the Senate must represent the authorities of the territories and carry out a function of control, in some matters, over the work of the State. In practice, however, this is not concretely and adequately anticipated. From the point of view of the reordering of legislative competences, the objective of the reform, once again widely shared, was to eliminate the confusion generated mainly by the category of competing subjects and to simplify the identification of their respective competences with clear and precise rules. However, the text proposed by the Government not only does not guarantee a uniquely interpretable discipline, but shifts the centre of gravity of legislative competences from the Regions to the State. Reading the articles of the reform that concern the tasks assigned to the state law in relation to the regional ones, we find the use of a multiplicity of formulas that could cause great interpretative problems: general and common provisions, coordination functions, general order profiles and still general principles, which become fundamental in other passages. How these expressions can contribute to the simplification of the identification of competences is not known. In addition, a clause is inserted that authorizes the law of the State, on a proposal of the Government, to intervene in matters not reserved to the exclusive legislation when it is required to protect the legal or economic unity of the Republic or of the national interest. It is a rule that creates further ambiguity and gives a new possibility for the State to invade the scope of regional competence. In summary, we are faced with a strong compression of the territorial autonomy: on the one hand, a poorer Senate of its powers to the advantage of a system clearly centralized on the institutional architecture, in which the Government would continue to play a role of absolute primacy; on the other hand, a reduction of the regional competences combined with the imposition of a clause of supremacy in favor of the Government. To conclude, in addition to a state imbalance caused by the reasons so far mentioned, there is concern about the growing inequality of treatment between regions and regions. I refer to the provision in Article 39, according to which the changes to Title V will apply to regions with special autonomy and autonomous provinces only after the revision of their respective statutes and on the basis of agreements between these regions and the State. This expresses yet another dramatic indifference towards those citizens and territories, contiguous to the Regions with a special statute, suffering from unequal treatment and opportunity. The introduction of this provision is devoid of logic, allowing, in fact, the Regions with a special statute - and we understand, unfortunately, the reason - and the Autonomous Provinces to negotiate in the second place additional forms of relief. In this way, the safeguard or supremacy clause - Article 120 of the Constitution refers to it as applicable in the Regions with special autonomy - is conditional, as far as its exercise is concerned, on what will subsequently be decided upon in the revision of the sources presiding over the same special autonomy. This is the indicator of a weak government, unable to resolve economic and social inequalities. The process of constitutional reform was a good opportunity to give a response to those territories, already in themselves disadvantaged by their economic, social and physical characteristics, mostly contiguous with territories that enjoy particular and beneficial forms of autonomy that aggravate an already complex situation. Who will allow this opportunity to be lost in nothing will have to assume the responsibility to explain to those territories and to those citizens the sense of legitimizing a deep difference in performance and opportunities - it is called competitive disadvantage or, if you like, unfair competition - between different areas of the national territory and, at the same time, of banning the principles of equality and guarantee instrumentally. [[Applause from the FI-PdL Group XVII]]

Moreover, the other particularity of this whole debate is that to the various issues, to the various criticisms, to the great because technicians on the fallacy of constitutional reform linked - as we know - also to the electoral law, there has not yet been a comprehensive response. No one, neither the members of the Government, nor the members of the majority who, in a truly disappointing and very minority way, have spoken here, even in this passage, have responded. It would have been useful to hear the objections, responses and replies to the sacred censorship of the constitutional reform system. The answer is not there. If we are to trust - which we do not do - what the President-in-Office of the Council tells us, we understand that this reform must be done because it has never been done, because it is a test of strength that he wants to impose on the country and perhaps in Europe to spend the card that he has managed to do what no one has been able to do before. So we are forced, despite ourselves, to follow you on a reform that is useless, harmful and above all dangerous because - as has already been explained several times - the real risk is in the arranged combination of the reform of the Senate and electoral law, the so-called Italicum that will come into force in a few months, which exposes the country to the risk of an authoritarian drift - and we have said this - to a concentration of powers and to the dismantling of all those systems of balancing and guarantee that the Constitution has so far guaranteed, good or bad, albeit distorted in practice. Then why all this? Why this authoritarian drift or, however, concentration of the power of the entire Italian political landscape towards a party or person? What can this be achieved for? The answers that, in my small, I have managed to give myself are in part those of a more general framework of enslavement even of Italy to much more complex and wide mechanisms, which have to do with the economic balances of the great world finance, as some of my colleague of the 5 Star Movement has just illustrated. Another logical answer could also be that this authoritarian drift, this concentration, the limitation of the spaces of democratic participation can only be functional to the attack and dismantling of another part of the Constitution, the one that has not yet been touched nominally, that is, the first part of the Constitution, in which there are the pillars of Western democracies, the rights of freedom, civil rights, freedom of thought, expression, association and the press. This is the legitimate fear that we remain after the criticisms made at the technical level in the various points and above all the lack of answers on the incongruity of the Senate in its composition, in its functions, on the chaotic situation that would be created if the plant really came into force and overcome - we trust that this does not happen - even the scrutiny of the referendum confirmation. And the reasons for the no will be explained very well - I trust - in all the squares and in all the places when that moment arrives. It is true, however, that this reform was born under the worst wishes. We have all been able to see that those who may today tear their clothes - and have done so in the past - pointing to shortcomings in internal democracy to political formations or heterodirections of parliamentary groups, have been the authors of events that have had - I believe - few precedents in parliamentary history. I am referring to the notorious action of the majority party, the Democratic Party, and the government, to those famous office replacements of two members of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, somewhat recalcitrant of the Renza reform system and therefore replaced. It seems to me that there has not been a great debate on the media and civil society in the face of the exercise of the imperative force of the party master, of the government and, at the moment, also of Italy. Those were the wishes in which all this reform project was born and, therefore, it was easy to see, since then, that the will was not at all reformative, really aimed at improving the legislative process or reducing the cost of the policy machine. It was the will to impose force, even by skillfully using the media system and most of it that still supports government action, to finish what seems to be - but will not be, because the referendum will say no, the Italians will say no to the majority - an authoritarian drift. Since everything has already been said by my colleagues and other members of the opposition forces in drawing the absurdity and danger of this reform, I thought I would use the rest of the time I was allowed to make a better contribution than I could in technical and critical terms. I would like to use the words of an appeal of the well-known jurist, perhaps among the most quoted and universally esteemed, Gustavo Zagrebelsky. I would like to conclude my speech, if time allows me, with the reading of what will be one of the reasons by which we will explain the no in the various squares of Italy, from now to the next few months. The functioning of democracy is difficult, close between inconclusion and force. Whoever believes that this is a battle that is fought once every five years at the time of the general elections and that, in the interval, everything is granted to you because you are the "winner," is wrong and is destined to be overwhelmed, sooner or later, by his pride or naivety, misplaced. The first victim of triumphalist illusion is Parliament. If we think that it is only a question of guaranteeing the action of those who "won the elections," Parliament must be the obedient support of this or of these: it must be an organ that executes the will of the Government. Otherwise, it is not only useless, but also counterproductive. The reforms in the field, in fact, are all directed towards the humiliation of Parliament, in its first function, the representative function. What do electoral laws mean, which provide for the choice of candidates through the "blocked lists" drawn up directly by the leaders of the parties or through the farce of the so-called "primary" if not the humiliation of that national function: triumph of the Gregatory spirit or of the voting market. The degraded product, if not damaged, is before everyone's eyes. Thus, while from the institutions it would be expected that they would be brought out of those who occupy her the best of themselves, or at least not the worst, in fact the opposite happens. These institutions lead to the bewilderment, submission, lack of ideas, availability to the powerful, the cowardice concerned or to their career or the authorisation to have a free hand in their affairs on the territory of reference. To be elected, these are the functional qualities of the party in which you enlist. You don't have to think you can make politics. It's not time anymore: time is executive! A clear and humiliating proof of parliamentary inanity is the story that has stirred political life in the last two years: the degradation of the Senate in the Secondary Chamber that should take place with the consent of the Senators. They are told - it has been told - "you are a cost, which does not correspond to any benefit; you are a burden of decision-making, which corresponds not to improvement, but to the deterioration of the quality of legislation. Yes, the Senate answers: that's the way it is." That is how the majority of this House will respond. "Until now we have been useless and harmful parasites and we are grateful to those who have made us aware of them! Delete us!" Let's look more closely at this case as a pitiful death manual or assisted suicide in constitutional life. To a non-superficial observer who does not stop at executive and "governability" rhetoric, i.e. at costs ("Free Senate," it has been said) and at speed (a resolution for each law, instead of two), the existence of a "second Chamber" is well founded on "conservatory reasons." Not conservative compared to the past, as it was at the time of the representative monarchies, when the question of the balancing of the anarchoid and dissipative tendencies of the elective Chamber, inclined because of its very nature, to squander money and traditions to attract voters. Then what you wanted to preserve was the legacy of the past. Today, in the face of the disaster of the waste society, it would be the opposite, i.e. conservative reasons for resources and opportunities for the future, to guarantee the generations to come. The Senate as conceived in the reform multiplies dissipation. It is intended to make an incongruous administrative projection of the second degree of local authorities, which in turn are hungry for public resources. This "administrative" perspective could have been opposed to a "constitutionalist." In the historical Senates, the conservative reasons corresponded to the appointment of director and duration of the office: two solutions, obviously impossible today, but easily replaceable with the election for an adequate duration, greater than the ordinary duration of the Chamber of Deputies, and with the rule of no re-eligibility, as a guarantee of independence from special contingent interests. Here I am opening a parenthesis: it has already been said that, among the many amending proposals of the 5 Star Movement, there were other possible solutions that would certainly have better achieved the stated objectives of this reform, the containment of expenditure, the improvement of legislative procedures, the guarantee of participation of citizens but - as we know - all this has not been. I keep reading: This could have been accompanied by particularly strict requirements of experience, competence and morality, contained in rules of incandidability, incompatibility and ineligibility measured on the nature of the tasks assigned to the elect. Fantasies. The constitutional reformers think of something else: to eliminate a political counterweight, to speed up the times. By not being able to eliminate, purely and simply, an organ, which, as it is considered useless, or even harmful, has been lost in a device whose absurd structural complication - the methods of extracting the new "senators" from the local assemblies - and processual - relations with the other Chamber - will come to light when it should be experienced the operation of the chamber. This was Gustavo Zagrebelsky's appeal, which is clearly reflected in what my colleagues and I have said several times. We trust in Italians, although today there are still few people who know what we are talking about, as can be seen from the data on the knowledge of the content of these reforms, which are still low. Certainly few Italians, apparently, are interested, because they rightly think of the other more immediate needs that Italian society needs. However, the time will have to come when we too will be forced to say that, on important matters, we have an idea and we have proposals. If we are forced to go down on the ground of the referendary battle, to defend the Constitution, which in some way guaranteed democratic principles, despite the upheavals of the Party in recent decades, we will be there to explain the reasons for the no and we believe that the Italians will listen to us well. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u135	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, Mr President-in-Office of the Government, I would like to address three aspects of this draft law, although it is a little effective to speak in this empty Chamber at this time, knowing that the games are already done, to the great frustration of all. The second concerns the new functions assigned to the Senate, as represented by the territorial institutions on the competences of the Regions to the probable litigation that will result. The last aspect concerns the costs of the new Senate. The Nazarene Pact, signed by President Berlusconi, has never been an agreement with prescriptive content, but a method of achieving a broad sharing on reforms, which, on the contrary, lacked by the will of the Democratic Party to adapt to its own convenience the changes to the Constitutional Charter and the electoral law. There are several changes - at least 17 have been identified - that have substantially modified that reform pact, directed towards a common and shared path. It should be stressed that a serious path had been taken to substantially change the rules of democracy in a shared way, not as you are doing now. Such a pact should not have been turned to party political logics because of the importance of the issues involved and the sum of the effects to be produced, and let us not forget the way in which this reform has now arrived in the Senate with the mercy of the presidencies of the Commissions that will vote in two days' time. The current Constitution is almost seventy years old and represents the cultural and political identity of our people, not just part of it, which is not the majority of the country. It, the result of an agreement, a compromise between the majority and the opposition of that time, guided democratic life and the political choices that affect our country. Precisely the path of the constituent assembly, even before the Committee of 75, has taught us that writing the Constitution means contributing to aggregation, sharing and not representing lacerations, conflicts or small dictatorships of low-profile characters. We are in the presence of a clear pride of the Government, thanks to a majority prize obtained by the Democratic Party - perhaps if the Government were to listen rather than continue reading and watching, but it is clear that it can do two things at the same time, compliments - a majority prize declared unconstitutional by the Constitutional Court. Thanks to a number of MPs, which was not the representation of the majority of the country at the time of the elections in 2013 and much less is now, the Democratic Party is trying to pass a series of reforms in a way, of course, by its nature, Bolshevik. And let us not forget the betrayal of 50 senators elected in this House who have passed for reasons of personal interest and certainly not for other interests for the country, betraying the mandate of voters. To these constitutional reforms in part, the PD has combined a rewriting of the electoral law, avoiding confrontation, avoiding the debate, placing the question of trust, precisely on the law that writes the rules of the game. An absurd, a unique case in the history of a democracy, now forgotten for the situations we are experiencing. And it is precisely by looking at this constitutional change and the rules of Italicum that a genuine suspension of democracy is born. A minority party in the country can win the elections, obtain a majority prize that allows it to govern, make appointments, elect most of the guarantee bodies, elect the President of the Republic. In this case the parliamentary minority, which, just as at this moment could be a numerical majority of the country, would be relegated to a marginal, insignificant role. There would be no sharing on the issues of great interest, real interest, for the citizens, which is instead fundamental for modernising the country. It remains a clever and high-sounding title, written only to deal with a confirmed referendum with extreme cleverness. In fact, when assessing the effects of the reform, there are no significant savings on policy costs. And there is no wonder, considering the umpteenth deficit manoeuvres made by this Government. According to the Department of Studies, Senate reform will save 90 million euros a year because the expenditure of the House of Deputies will increase by more than 60 million euros a year, where despite its usual title, reform, the number of Members has remained exactly the same. Was it enough to save money on the stationery? The procedure for designating the new Senate remains confusing and will certainly require a number of implementing rules. The role of representatives of the Regions in the Senate is however elusive. It is expected that senators, who are also councillors or presidents of the Region, or even mayors, will be present without clarifying how they will fully play their roles, and always about savings, everything that can be spared by the Senate will be spent by the local authorities on the diary and maintenance of the new senators. The functions attributed to the new Senate are inconsistent, as it ends the bicameralism parity, it was enough to change the Regulations to speed up the process, but it is attributed a series of powers that make it resemble the unified Conference. Then someone, perhaps Minister Boschi, said the other day: At this point the State-Regions Conference is useless. However, we forget that the unified conference costs much less than the new Senate, which would have been better closed. In general, as we have said, the costs of politics do not really decrease. The division of competences between the State and the Regions leads to a neocentralism that will certainly produce a new and articulated constitutional litigation, this time promoted not by the State, but by the Regions. This is a reform that does not tackle in a serious and articulated way the real challenges for the modernization of the country. ParlaMint-IT_2016-01-19-LEG17-Senato-sed-562.ana.u137	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen of the senators, today the last act is being carried out in which the Government intends to put a blind eye to the participatory democracy of this country. Neither the comments made by a significant number of parliamentarians in the previous passages in the Chambers, nor the externation of authoritative opinions, have served to bring back on their own steps this Government which, at any cost, without looking at the result of this mess, has decided to modify our Constitutional Charter and precisely the role, functions and methods of election of the members of the Senate. The Senate of the Republic, in fact, will not be abolished, contrary to what so far many have understood, but simply delegitimized in its role and this will happen at the hands of a Parliament also illegitimate, as it had already declared the renowned judgment of the Constitutional Court that has pronounced itself on the constitutional legitimacy of the Porcellum. It will not happen, and there will be no significant savings in expenditure from one billion to about 500 million and today, instead, it has been quantified in 80 million euros. Although the 5 Star Movement is always at the forefront in order to achieve the increasing reduction in public spending, the savings achieved by the transformation of the Senate would not be adequate compared to the deficits that would suffer the processes of participation of democracy, lacking the vital functions that the Senate has carried out until today. From the very beginning, we were sceptical about the surreal motives that prompted the government to empty the Senate of all its prerogatives. The cause of all evil, according to governmental sources, is the perfect bicameralism which, in their opinion, makes the legislative process cumbersome, causing unjustified delays in the parliamentary process and, in some cases, even causing backward steps rather than forwards. With a loud voice you have tried to inculcate us that the governability of a country cannot depend on the presence of two Chambers with equal powers that, by virtue of their eternal opposition, do not allow the country to go on. Nothing more wrong. I would like to remind you that there are not too many times when the double reading, necessary for the approval of legislative texts, has prevented the completion of abuses, such as blocking the dangerous reform of Article 138 of the Constitution or the rule that would have sanctioned the municipalities anti-slot machines or, as has been the case in the past legislature, the blocking of the Alfano praise in its first formulation or the approval of the so-called gag law. Even more serious circumstance is that, however, with this reform, bicameralism will not be overcome. As supported by authoritative jurists, the Senate model provided by the current text is not suitable to overcome the bicameralism of equality in the direction of an effective territorial counterweight within the National Parliament, but would configure a Political Assembly with fewer functions, competences and number of members compared to the Chamber of Deputies and therefore with lower democratic legitimacy, but with all the existing begas in the regional political micro-systems. Not to mention the scandals involving local politics, which would be more than enough reason to be against the Senate elected by the regional councils. It would have been more appropriate to take the path of monocameralism, provided that strong internal counterpowers were foreseen, setting the Senate as a mere consultative body without legislative powers. Authors continue to argue that this reform, as well as a mess, is certainly a mess. It would have sufficed to pay more attention to the content of the judgment of the Constitutional Court which declared Porcellum illegitimate to understand that the path taken was wrong. In this regard, it is useful here to recall the work of analysis and comparison carried out in this country by the members of the committees to defend the Constitution, who have already anticipated their referendary battle to prevent the amendment of the Constitution, as well as wanting to repeal the Italicum. According to the Constitutional Court, this Parliament, following the unconstitutionality of the electoral system that led to the election of all present, should have only continued to operate, thanks to the application of the fundamental principle of the continuity of the State, for a limited period. Certainly today he would not have the legitimacy to go so far as to amend the Constitutional Charter. However, wishing to enter into the merit of the reform, it certainly stands out the elimination of internal and external counterpowers and this in contempt of the fundamental principles that inspired our founding Fathers. From the point of view of functions and the number of components, the imbalance between both rooms is absolutely uncompromising. The so-called Senate of Autonomies could no longer prevent the completion of a legislative process by promoting substantial changes, nor could the small number of members in joint meetings be considered decisive in any vote. In addition, none of the senators will be more an expression of a popular vote, violating - in doing so - one of the fundamental principles of the Constitution, namely that provided for in Article 1, which bases our democracy on popular participation. Another serious vice that characterizes this reform is clearly the shift of the axis in the relationship between Government and Parliament, having purposely preferred governance rather than representativeness. From now on, the government will dictate the agenda for parliamentary work. Thanks to Italicum, in fact, the majority of the members of the House will be composed of the elected party that won the elections, even if it will represent a minority in the country. The Senate, on the other hand, will consist of part-time regional councillors, expression of a regional vote, and mayors who will certainly be busy solving the indisputable local problems. No guarantee will be given to minorities within their own country who will not even have the power to request the establishment of committees of inquiry in the state. It is quite clear that the reform of the Senate, together with the reform of the electoral law, will transfer all power to a single political force, if not to a single person, who will have both the control of the House, having obtained the majority of the seats thanks to the majority prize, and the control of the Executive. The government can operate without interference. Long live governability! These observations should lead each of us to contribute so that this text, as it has been conceived, will never see the light, because its path has suffered many interferences from the Government. Given what kind of action is being taken to the detriment of democracy, it seems surprising that this Government, then, expects to barter margins of flexibility, in terms of budgetary policy, with the implementation of this reform that - in his opinion - will allow the country to restart. In all institutional fora we have always argued that there are other reforms on which we should focus, such as the law on conflict of interest and anti-corruption, in order to put in motion a sector now considered by all to be a failure. We should rather reactivate the attention and trust of foreign capital. The 5 Star Movement has repeatedly expressed its opinion on how to reform Parliament in its entirety, without distorting its constitutional balance, reducing the costs of politics in fact. Parliament, of course, in its composition, should be kept clean of convicted persons, who cannot be candidates or elected; moreover, it must include the limit of two parliamentary mandates, so as to allow for a continuous replacement of the national political class. As for the cuts in the costs of politics that the Government continues to announce, the 5 Star Movement has already shown that this is possible, and even immediately, through the half-wage of salaries, the compulsory reporting of the diary and the return of what is not spent, the abolition of the office allowance, the abolition of the end-of-term cheque beyond, clearly, the actual abolition of public funding to parties. It is not difficult to do all this; it depends only on us and our conscience. Our battle won't end with this vote. We, like others, are already ready to mobilize anyone who still cares about the democracy of this country and still believe in the fundamental principles that our founding fathers have handed down to us with the Constitutional Charter. I conclude with a passage taken from Luigi Sturzo's 1957 speech, according to which: The Constitution is the foundation of the Republic. If it falls from the heart of the people, if it is not respected by the political authorities, if it is not defended by the government and Parliament, if it is tampered with by the parties, the hard ground on which our institutions are manufactured and anchored our freedoms will be lost. Let's make sure we don't lose democratic ground under our feet. [[Applause from the M5S Group.]]
